#5036. Binance hack may be ‘temporary setback’ for crypto, Grayscale’s Pandl says

Zach Pandl highlighted institutional custody safeguards as crypto markets recovered some losses following the record $6.3 billion Binance hack.

Binance’s $6.3 billion hack could temporarily hurt confidence in crypto markets but would ultimately lead to stronger security practices across exchanges, according to Grayscale.

“It also underscores the importance of institutional custody, such as offered by US-based crypto [exchange-traded products],” said Grayscale’s head of research, Zach Pandl, in a Tuesday report.

“These vehicles hold their assets at state-chartered banks and qualified custodians, typically offering offline storage, multi-signature wallets, SOC attestations and insurance policies,” he added.

Binance suffered a record $6.3 billion hack Thursday and pledged to cover all customer losses. The FBI attributed the attack to North Korea. Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the allegations as “absurd rhetoric.”

The hack was followed by a market sell-off, with Bitcoin falling below $77,000 and BNB losing more than 8% over 24 hours by early Friday.

By Wednesday, total crypto market capitalization had rebounded 2% over the past 24 hours to about $2.63 trillion, after touching a weekly low of about $2.55 trillion earlier in the day. The recovery left the market about $100 billion below its Oct. 28 level.

Bitwise’s head of research, André Dragosch, told Cointelegraph that recent outflows from US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds were “related to a short-term risk-off environment rather than a structural change in institutional sentiment.”

#5035. Morning Minute: CFTC Clears Stock Perps, Gets Sued the Same Day

Morning Minute: CFTC Clears Stock Perps, Gets Sued the Same Day

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

⚖️ CFTC Clears Stock Perps, Gets Sued the Same Day

A perpetual future is a leveraged bet on a price that never expires. You hold it as long as you can meet margin and keep paying a funding rate. It’s analog product would be options in TradFi, with a few differences (primarily no expiration date). Crypto traders have used them for years, mostly on offshore venues like Binance and onchain platforms like Hyperliquid, because U.S. law had no clear home for the product.

On Tuesday the CFTC said Americans can now trade them on individual stocks.

Hours later, Wall Street sued to stop it.

The approval came as a statement from CFTC Chairman Michael Selig. Equity perpetual futures are now tradable on CFTC-registered exchanges, classified as security futures products under the Commodity Futures Modernization Act of 2000, which puts them under joint CFTC and SEC oversight.

The list of venues that can actually do this is short, and it was assembled quietly. A firm needs to be a CFTC-designated contract market and separately notice-registered with the SEC as a national securities exchange for security futures products. Seven entities hold both today: Cboe, CME, Coinbase, Kalshi, Bitnomial, Nadex, and ICE Futures U.S. Five of those seven filed inside a single 30-day window in September.

Kalshi is furthest along, with a filing covering 56 to 58 names and a launch expected within days. Coinbase has filed to list perps on Apple, Tesla and Nvidia. So those two are immediate winners. But Hyperliquid and Lighter, the top crypto perps protocols, are next in line for potential CFTC approvals and are likely future winners as well (LIT +10% on the day).

Then came the lawsuit. A new group called the Capital Markets Alliance filed in the Southern District of New York, naming Cboe, Citadel Securities and the Healthy Markets Association among its members. Its argument runs on two tracks.

The group wants the policy eliminated. The American Securities Association piled on separately, pointing out that Congress banned selling security-based swaps to retail investors at all.

A CFTC spokesman called the suit “troubling,” adding that “our markets should be governed by law, not litigation campaigns intended to block innovation.” The CME already tried this once, suing over the CFTC’s crypto perps approval on the theory that perpetuals are swaps rather than futures. That one went nowhere.

Well, remember that old phrase: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, then you win."

We’re at the fight stage. Next stop, winning.

#5031. Weighted Daggers. Sword Yoga. Real-Life Gauntlets. Welcome to the World of Romantasy-Inspired Fitness.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

You’ll still break a sweat, but with “the added bonus of feeling badass.”

The female warrior lunges, her sword slashing in the sunlit forest. Clad in black armor, she spins, whirling her weapon over her head. Silver slices the air. She jumps, spins, then kicks. No, this isn’t a Valkyrie training scene from the newest installment in Sarah J. Maas’s hit romantasy (romantic fantasy) series, A Court of Splintered Harmony. It’s a workout class.

Charity LeBlanc didn’t set out to create a romantasy-inspired workout program. Originally, the former American Ninja Warrior semifinalist just wanted to share videos of her fitness journey and fantasy-inspired cosplay creations on social media—and her follower count grew. Then, in 2024, she heard about the Dragon Gauntlet, a Fourth Wing-inspired obstacle course race in Boyne Falls, Michigan. She asked her followers if they wanted to train together for the event, and designed a workout program that combined strength training, HIIT cardio, combat circuits, and weapons training. “It wasn't just working out to do a rep; it was to gain a skill,” says LeBlanc. “And it took off.”

Romantasy-inspired workout programs have exploded over the last few years, mirroring the genre’s takeover of the publishing industry. After reading A Court of Thorns and Roses in 2022, trainer Samantha Willens put her Harry Potter- and Star Wars-themed Pilates classes on pause and shifted her focus to workouts inspired by the bestselling romantasy series. She created the Valkyrie Squad Program, a four-month fitness plan based on the series’ fifth book, A Court of Silver Flames—and her business, Micro Squad, grew exponentially.

In 2024, Sabina Storberg launched WeaponUp, a sword yoga platform, thinking it would attract women into traditional martial arts or yoga. But “it was actually the book girls,” she says. Now, they have thousands of members around the world.

Sure, gaining the endurance of a dragon rider, the agility of a half-Fae assassin, or the muscle of a Valkyrie warrior (IYKYK!) isn’t your average fitness goal, but that’s why these workouts work. Romantasy books allow women to fall in love with stories of female empowerment—and these workouts allow women to live out those stories in real life. “There's something to be said for channeling a character you admire, especially one who embodies strength, confidence, and resilience,” says exercise physiologist Rachelle Reed, PhD. “For some women, that connection might make exercise feel more fun and approachable, or even help them feel more confident trying something new.”

Here’s how women are taking on their own version of a heroine’s journey—and what mixing sweat with a little bit of magic has done for their minds and bodies.

Jamie Dalton, 38, never really enjoyed working out, especially in front of other people. She always struggled with coordination, which made her self-conscious. “Weight training was the closest to me enjoying something because that required the least amount of coordination,” Dalton says. “But it just felt more like a punishment, and punishments, you don't keep doing long-term.” She’d often start a workout program only to stop a few months later.

But when Dalton discovered WeaponUp, her relationship with fitness transformed. The classes consist of sword drills and choreographed flows inspired by romantasy characters, like Nesta Archeron from ACOTAR and Manon Blackbeak from Throne of Glass. And while the workouts are rooted in fantasy, the moves are a real fusion of kung-fu and yoga, and involve a lot of squats, lunges, kicks, core work, balance, and mobility. They also present a mental challenge as you learn to wield a sword with each hand and on either side of the body. Five months in, and Dalton is still going strong with WeaponUp, training four to five times a week.

While working out wasn’t totally foreign to Dalton, many of the people who sign up for WeaponUp, Whimsy Forge, and Micro Squad are new to exercise. Romantasy-inspired fitness “lowers the barrier to entry” because it speaks to people’s existing interests and feels personalized; they can put themselves in the shoes of the strong female protagonist(s) they’ve admired in book form, Reed says.

For instance, all of Whimsy Forge’s eight-week workout plans, called character arcs, are designed to emulate a different fantasy character's abilities, whether that's an Elven Huntress’ agility, a Dragon Rider’s endurance, or a Valkyrie Warrior’s strength. Whichever workout plan you choose, you’ll still do classic exercise moves, such as squats and presses, as well as optional special skill challenges that require a (play) weapon. “I'm really passionate about making sure the programs are accessible, and I am not a gym girl,” says LeBlanc. “For a lot of the workouts, all you really need is a yoga mat and some space.”

Both the level of access and the genuine enthusiasm for the subject matter are enough to help some people get started with fitness, which is often the hardest part. “When people are new exercisers, if they have an enjoyable or interesting experience the first few times, it's easier for them to start building toward a habit,” says Reed. “Eventually, your discipline and intrinsic motivation pick up.”

Of course, romantasy-inspired fitness can also help more experienced exercisers get out of a workout rut. Brooke Lydon, 33, used to love taking fitness classes, but between a full-time job and raising her 4-year-old daughter, she didn’t have the time. A fan of yoga teacher Taylor Johnson, Lydon thought that doing her fantasy-inspired strength and mobility program, Arius, might be a fun way to get back into fitness, and she liked that she could do the program at home.

Plus, it gave Lydon an extra opportunity to listen to her romantasy audiobooks during workouts, which doubled as a form of motivation. She’d often imagine herself competing against her favorite characters and end up working harder. “I would be like, ‘If she can do it, I have to do it,’” she says.

Romantasy-inspired fitness may offer an extra incentive to exercise, but it’s also just plain fun. Each program infuses a sense of childlike joy and wonder into workouts. “We don't have that many fun fitness experiences available to us—everything is for a goal,” Storberg says. “I'm going to go to the gym so I gain muscle, and that's very important, but we don't make it fun like we used to when we were children.”

Can a romantasy workout provide the same level of results as traditional strength training or cardio? For Dalton, who was losing muscle and experiencing joint pain due to perimenopause, the answer is a resounding yes. “I definitely feel like I've regained some of the mobility that I felt like I was losing, and I've definitely regained a lot of the strength back,” she says.

WeaponUp classes have also helped Dalton improve her coordination, thanks to the focus on proprioception (the understanding of where your body is in space). “You're doing your whole flow on a yoga mat, and you might have your kids' stuff in one corner, so you have to have a really good understanding of where you are in space, where the sword is in space,” says Storberg.

But sword yoga also provides balance training in a more accessible way, especially since you have a prop. “When I try to do a squat normally, I feel like I'm going to fall over,” Dalton says. “But because of the flow that they have you do with a sword, somehow it counterbalances so I feel more grounded and secure.”

Meanwhile, in Micro Squad’s Pilates-based classes, you’ll do moves like Russian twist punches with (optional) Dragon’s Edge weighted daggers that evoke a character or pivotal scene from ACOTAR through the series called “A Fortnight in Prythian.” As such, you’ll experience many of the same muscle endurance benefits that come from any low-weight, high-rep workout. But there’s also “the added bonus of feeling badass while wielding weighted daggers and immersing yourself in the world,” Willens says.

Additionally, in the Valkyrie Squad program, you’ll train foundational strength and mobility skills before progressing to martial arts, kickboxing drills, and sword training, like the warrior characters in A Court of Silver Flames. Many Micro Squad members finish the program feeling not only confident in their bodies, but also like they can take on any obstacle—physical or mental, Willens says.

It’s no coincidence that a fitness program based in fantasy can lead to real-life gains. If you can build confidence with a class you find interesting, you’re more likely to stick with it for the long-term, Reed says. In fact, several WeaponUp members have lost 50 pounds or more after starting the program. “That got them into a trajectory of getting into wellness [and] being able to really take their health back,” Storberg adds. “There's so many women that are actually sticking to a fitness routine because it's fun, unique, and different.”

Badassery is the intangible benefit that all romantasy-themed workouts seem to share. “A lot of women have never seen themselves as this person who is strong, who is capable, who can do skills, but walking into these programs where they feel like they're becoming something, that transformation is real for them,” LeBlanc says. “They actually are becoming stronger.”

With the popularity of romantasy fitness only growing, it’s also evident that more women are invested in what their bodies can do, not what they look like. For as much as the average romantasy female main character may uphold traditional beauty standards, that’s not why millions of readers love them, or why thousands want to work out like them. “It's not just about getting a smaller waist or growing glutes. A lot of women's fitness is about how you look and less about how it makes you feel,” says LeBlanc. “[This is] a lot more motivating and empowering.”

Romantasy-inspired fitness has even helped Dalton navigate discussions with her 7-year-old daughter about exercise, food, and health in general. “It's made it a lot easier to turn that conversation into becoming strong and powerful, instead of skinny,” she says. Her daughter will often join her for WeaponUp workouts, and “that has been a really cool thing for me as a mom,” Dalton says. “I want my kid to exercise in a way that's going to help her build healthy habits, and I love that this is letting me do that with her.”

Fantasy fitness is becoming more than just a workout trend—it’s a way for fans to connect beyond the pages of their favorite books. Whimsy Forge hosts a semi-annual Trial of the Guilds, a community-wide virtual fitness challenge where members choose one of the aforementioned character arcs, and everyone in the same arc becomes a Guild, or team. Then, all the Guilds compete against each other for eight weeks. “We do it as a community, so you feel like you're with friends and people that are going to encourage you,” says LeBlanc.

In addition to online fitness programming, several of these platforms now offer in-person classes and meetups. The WeaponUp team just finished a 10-city Graceful Warrior tour that took them from coast to coast, attracting a range of ages, sizes, and fitness abilities, says Storberg: “What they have in common is this desire to be the main character.” That desire is so strong for Dalton that she’s willing to drive three or four hours to attend an in-person WeaponUp event. “[I’d] go and make a whole day of it,” she says.

The collective power of the book girls, both online and in-person, is not to be underestimated. “Having a community can provide accountability, encouragement, and a sense of belonging, which can all make it easier to stick with an exercise routine over time,” says Reed. “But what's particularly interesting here is that these women are connecting over more than just exercise. They already have a shared interest in the books, characters, and stories, which gives them another way to connect with one another.” (Perhaps unsurprisingly, Whimsy Forge, WeaponUp, and Micro Squad all boast virtual book clubs.)

While many women are initially drawn to romantasy fitness because of a connection to a character, they not only forge new connections with other readers, but ultimately, themselves. “It’s less about acting like some of these female main characters that we see in the books, but rather becoming a female main character in your own life,” says Storberg. After all, who needs a shadow daddy when you can swing a sword?

#5028. Crédit Mutuel Arkéa Confirms Cyberattack After ZachXBT Ties It to $133M Crypto Theft

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa Confirms Cyberattack After ZachXBT Ties It to $133M Crypto Theft

French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has confirmed that it was hit by a cyberattack that allowed fraudulent operations, a day after onchain investigator ZachXBT named it as the alleged victim of a crypto theft of more than $133 million.

In a statement shared with Decrypt on Wednesday, the bank said the incident was "closed and now under control," and that operations were resuming gradually after it put stronger security measures in place. It said customers' assets were not affected, and that the attack had only delayed the execution of some transfers.

The bank did not mention crypto or say how much was taken. The amounts affected are still being assessed, but are "in any event not of a nature to call into question the solidity and financial health of the group," it said, according to Decrypt's translation.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa said it informed the relevant supervisory authorities as soon as it detected the attack, and temporarily suspended some transactions as a precaution to prevent further fraudulent movements. It also warned customers to watch out for phishing attempts following the attack. Decrypt has reached out to Crédit Mutuel Arkéa for further comment, including on whether the losses involved crypto.

"Disclosing the EU financial institution exploited for $133M+ is allegedly Credit Mutuel Arkea, a French bank with $235B in assets," ZachXBT wrote on his Telegram channel on Tuesday. "The attack on the bank occurred for multiple days. I suspect the amount stolen is larger when accounting for OTC activity."

In an update on Tuesday, he said he had identified a further $27 million in exchange withdrawals by the attacker, bringing the total to more than $133 million, and that some of those withdrawals took place after his initial disclosure. He also published four Ethereum addresses he said were linked to the theft.

"My working theory is a large financial institution in Western Europe was exploited and it's currently being covered up," ZachXBT wrote at the time, accusing it of not having conducted "proper incident disclosure" and thereby preventing law enforcement from freezing some $30 million in stolen funds.

He did not say how he had identified the bank, or how the attacker gained access to its exchange accounts.

The mutual banking group has been preparing to move into crypto. In May, French financial newspaper L'Agefi reported that it had applied for a license under MiCA, the EU's crypto rulebook, to offer crypto services to its retail customers and to provide them as a white-label service through its Arkéa Banking Services division.

The allegation comes days after the $6.3 billion hack of Binance, the largest crypto theft on record, which the FBI has attributed to North Korean hackers. ZachXBT said the attacker in the suspected EU theft remains unidentified, but that he could say "with a high degree of confidence it is NOT Lazarus Group," the North Korean hacking collective, "based on multiple laundering indicators."

This story has been updated to add comment from Crédit Mutuel Arkéa.

#5026. Canton’s CC rises 4% as SEC staff view it as ‘digital commodity’

SEC staff’s no-action letter covers CC earned by running infrastructure and applications, but is not a binding ruling on its legal status.

Canton Network’s native cryptocurrency rose 4% on Wednesday after the US Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) Division of Corporation Finance issued a no-action letter stating it views the token as a “digital commodity.”

Canton Coin (CC) traded at $0.098 at 10:27 am UTC, but was still down roughly 4.2% over the past week, according to CoinMarketCap.

In its Tuesday letter, SEC staff accepted the explanation that CC’s value comes from how the network operates and demand for the token, rather than a central team’s efforts to generate profits for holders.

The division also said it would not recommend enforcement action over CC’s issuance without securities registration, provided the tokens are created under the conditions described by Canton.

In Friday’s letter to the SEC, Canton developer Digital Asset said network participants earn CC by operating infrastructure and applications, with the protocol determining how many tokens they can mint based on their activity.

The SEC issued guidance in March distinguishing digital commodities, such as Bitcoin and Ether, from securities. It said these tokens are not themselves securities, although the way they are offered and sold can still be subject to securities laws.

The SEC’s response reflects its staff’s position and is not a binding ruling on CC’s legal status.

SEC staff previously issued token-related no-action letters to crypto projects, including DoubleZero (2Z) in September 2025 and Solana-native decentralized physical infrastructure network (DePIN) project Fuse in November 2025.

#5025. Raw Denim vs. Regular Denim: Which Is Actually Worth Buying?

Here’s what to know about both kinds of jeans.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I COME FROM the denim era where streetwear juggernauts like FUBU and Rocawear reigned supreme, along with mall favorites like Abercrombie & Fitch and Hollister. I was in my preteens rocking all these clothing brands and you couldn’t tell me their baggy-stacking-at-the-shoe jeans weren’t the flyest on the market. Since I began working as a fashion editor, I’ve practically worn every pair of jeans in the denim universe. Slim, relaxed, stretch, washed, distressed—you name it, I’ve probably tried it. I’ve even tried my hand at denim-making in Japan, the mecca of old-school, high-quality denim, where I produced my own pair of selvedge jeans. Denim is one of those rare items in a guy’s wardrobe that never goes out of style.

And after years of being in the menswear space, I’ve gotten familiar with the nuances that distinguish one kind of denim from the next. Two worth knowing are regular denim and raw denim. Raw denim usually looks darker, stiffer, and more uniform in color, while regular denim has a softer feel and a more broken-in appearance with visible fading or whiskering from factory washing and finishing. Those differences also affect how each pair feels when you first put it on and how it changes over the years. Here’s what to know about both and which one deserves a spot in your rotation.

Regular jeans are usually made by weaving cotton yarn into denim, then cutting fabric into patterns and sewing them together to create the finished garment. These jeans have already gone through washing or finishing treatments to soften the fabric and give them that broken-in look, so you don’t have to spend weeks waiting for them to feel comfortable. If I want to throw on a pair of jeans and get on with my day, this is the kind I’m reaching for. I also like that you can find them in practically every fit and wash, whether you’re after a clean, dark indigo pair or something faded and lived-in. Most everyday options from brands like Levi’s and Wrangler run roughly $40 to $100, while designer and premium styles can cost considerably more. For me, prewashed denim makes the most sense when you want that comfortable feel from the jump. There’s nothing wrong with skipping the break-in period and getting straight to the good part.

I like raw denim because you get to see how jeans change the more you wear them. Unlike prewashed jeans, raw denim typically hasn’t been washed after the dyeing and manufacturing process, so it typically arrives stiff, dark, and pretty rigid. One thing to watch out for, though, is dye transfer. Indigo can rub off onto lighter-colored clothes, shoes, and other materials, including suede. I’ve had this happen before, so I'd be mindful of what you wear with a fresh pair. But that’s part of the appeal for denim enthusiasts. As you wear it, the fabric softens and develops fades and creases based on how you move, sit, and live in your jeans. You’re essentially breaking them in yourself, which makes the finished look feel uniquely yours. Prices can start around $60 to $100 for entry-level options and climb to $150 to $300 or more for premium denim, particularly Japanese-made styles. I’ll admit, the break-in period takes some patience, but if you enjoy watching your jeans evolve over time, raw denim is worth considering.

Raw denim and selvedge denim get lumped together all the time, but they actually refer to different things. Raw denim describes the fabric’s finishing state: It’s typically sold without a post-production wash that softens the fabric or creates a faded look. Selvedge, on the other hand, refers to how the denim is woven, creating a clean, self-finished edge you can often spot by rolling up the cuff. You can have raw selvedge denim, washed selvedge denim, or raw denim that isn’t selvedge at all. Having made my own pair of selvedge jeans in Japan, I’ve seen firsthand how the construction differs. Once you know what to look for, the distinction is pretty easy to spot.

At the end of the day, I don’t think you need to drop $300 on raw denim to dress well. After years of working in menswear, I’ve learned that a higher price tag doesn’t automatically make something the right choice for your wardrobe. I’ve seen guys look just as good in an affordable pair of Levi’s as they do in premium Japanese denim. What matters more is finding comfortable jeans that fit your body and work with the clothes you already own. I’m always going to appreciate the quality craftsmanship behind a great pair of raw denim, but there’s no single right way to wear jeans. I think every guy should have one dependable pair, whether that’s raw or prewashed. But ultimately, like most things in style, it comes down to personal preference—and knowing what works for you.

#5020. Wall Street’s Plumbing Goes Onchain as Morgan Stanley Tokenizes Securities

Wall Street’s Plumbing Goes Onchain as Morgan Stanley Tokenizes Securities

Morgan Stanley has started moving U.S. stocks and Treasuries onto blockchain rails, using Wall Street’s own plumbing to do it.

The bank said Tuesday that it’s among the first to tokenize real-world assets through the DTCC Tokenization Service, converting U.S. equities and Treasuries into tokenized form.

The service, which went live Oct. 28, is run by the Depository Trust Co., a subsidiary of the Depository Trust & Clearing Corp. The DTC is the post-trade backbone of U.S. markets, with more than $114 trillion in assets under custody.

“The hands-on experience of integrating tokenized securities with established market infrastructure will inform the continued development of the firm’s digital asset capabilities as the market evolves,” said Amy Oldenburg, Morgan Stanley’s head of digital assets strategy.

The service lets eligible firms convert securities held at DTC into tokenized “digital twins” and back again, delivering them to digital wallets of their choice. The tokens keep the same ownership rights, investor protections and economic entitlements as the originals, according to DTCC, and are designed to let firms move collateral and tap new pools of liquidity within minutes. The service currently runs on the Canton Network and DTCC’s private Besu network, with more blockchains planned for 2027.

Morgan Stanley is one of 20 DTC participants that have signed up so far, alongside Bank of America, Citi, J.P. Morgan, Fidelity Investments, Citadel Securities, and UBS, among others. DTCC said it developed the service with input from more than 150 firms.

The launch caps a regulatory path that began in December 2025, when the SEC gave DTC a no-action letter allowing it to tokenize Russell 1000 stocks, major index ETFs and U.S. Treasuries. DTCC has also worked with Chainlink on tokenized collateral management and laid out its plans for the service earlier this year as major Wall Street firms signed on.

The bank-led approach contrasts with crypto-native efforts to tokenize stocks. Fifty-six companies, including Tesla, SpaceX, Amazon, and Nvidia, have objected to having their shares traded on OKXICE TSV, a planned tokenized stock venue from OKX and NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange. Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, those objections bar the venue from listing third-party tokenized versions of their stock.

Demand is building, but the market is still young. The International Monetary Fund found last month that tokenized stocks trade mostly outside U.S. market hours, though they remain more volatile and less liquid than their traditional counterparts.

#5013. France backs reporting for self-custodied crypto worth 100K euros or more

The amendment would require taxpayers to report holdings worth at least $112,000, but the measure still needs to survive the budget process.

The French National Assembly approved an amendment that would require taxpayers to report self-custodied crypto holdings totaling at least 100,000 euros ($112,000) to tax authorities.

Amendment I-2701 was adopted on Tuesday in a 150–44 vote, with eight abstentions, according to the Assembly’s official voting record. The threshold would be assessed on Dec. 31 of the relevant tax year.

Separately, the Assembly rejected an amendment that would have exempted gains on crypto payments for goods and services from tax if the payments totaled no more than 1,000 euros ($1,120) during the tax year. The proposal was defeated 69–14, according to the Assembly’s transcript.

The reporting requirement forms part of the 2027 Finance Bill and would need to be retained in the final legislation to become law. The Assembly faces a Nov. 17 deadline to complete its first reading of the bill. Senate debate is scheduled to begin Nov. 20, according to its schedule.

The National Assembly postponed Tuesday’s scheduled vote on the budget’s revenue section for the second time as lawmakers continued debating amendments. The vote was originally scheduled for Oct. 20.

The vote’s postponement itself does not introduce a significant change, but the “likely rejection” of the budget’s revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments,” Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph.

On Friday, the Assembly rejected two proposals that would have applied exit taxes to unrealized crypto gains when taxpayers transferred their tax residence abroad.

Lawmakers also approved an amendment on Oct. 16 that would tax gains from converting crypto into stablecoins. The proposal covers stablecoins classified as electronic money tokens under EU rules and would still need to be retained in the final law.

#5012. Bitcoin Rebounds After Democratic Midterm Sweep, But Crypto Faces Tougher Fight in Washington

Bitcoin Rebounds After Democratic Midterm Sweep, But Crypto Faces Tougher Fight in Washington

Bitcoin and Ethereum recovered Wednesday after Democrats won control of both houses of Congress in the U.S. midterm elections, despite the results likely making the crypto industry's push for friendlier federal rules more difficult.

The crypto market recovery, in technical terms, appears to be more a natural bounce, and in line with expectations, instead of a trend reversal or a FOMO-driven spike. The overnight selloff also reversed as U.S. stock futures bounced back, making it hard to pin the recovery on politics alone.

Bitcoin briefly hit a six-week low before a bounce that took it back to just above $80,000 once again, above its average price of the last 50 days, which is a solid bullish sign after weeks of bearish correction.

Ethereum, meanwhile, spiked 7% to $2,434, while U.S. stocks on the whole jumped around 1.6%, with the Dow Jones Industrial Average leading the way with a 2.5% rally.

Analysts who spoke to Decrypt said the Democratic sweep had largely been priced into crypto markets before election night. A rebound in Bitcoin therefore says less about investors welcoming the election result than about how much bad news they were already expecting.

The industry has spent more than $300 million supporting crypto-friendly candidates from both parties across the 2024 and 2026 election cycles. About $176 million went toward more than 100 races this year alone.

That spending was aimed partly at getting legislation through Congress that would give digital asset businesses clearer rules. The Clarity Act, a major piece of that effort, would define how federal regulators oversee different types of crypto assets and trading activity.

The bill was in trouble before Tuesday's election. Senators voted 49-50 against advancing it in September, falling short of the 60 votes needed to move past a procedural hurdle. That was not a final vote on the legislation, but it stopped the measure from reaching a full debate.

Negotiations had already run into disputes over restrictions on government officials' crypto business interests and the treatment of rewards paid on stablecoins, tokens designed to hold a fixed value. President Donald Trump's personal and family crypto ventures became a particular point of conflict for Democratic lawmakers.

Democratic control of Congress makes a quick return to the same legislative deal less likely, but it does not automatically undo what the industry secured under Trump. His administration has eased enforcement, established a strategic Bitcoin reserve and overseen the passage of a federal stablecoin law.

The distinction matters more for exchanges, token issuers, and other firms seeking clear legal permissions than for Bitcoin's day-to-day price. Analysts told Decrypt that liquidity, interest rates and investment flows still exert a stronger influence on the cryptocurrency itself.

The broader crypto market has shed roughly $300 billion over the past month, bringing its total value to about $2.67 trillion. Rising borrowing costs and worries about returns on artificial intelligence investments have pushed traders toward less risky assets like bonds or gold.

Major Bitcoin exchange-traded funds, which offer exposure to the asset through traditional brokerage accounts, recorded $215 million in net withdrawals in October. That brought their year-to-date net outflows to $1.84 billion.

The new Congress takes office in January 2027. Unless lawmakers revive the Clarity Act before the current session ends, the industry will need to start a new legislative effort with Democrats controlling both chambers.

#5009. Influencers Are Doing ‘Olive Oil Shots.’ We Asked 4 Experts If That’s a Good Idea—and They All Basically Said the Same Thing.

The truth about the supposed health practice—revealed.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

SO OLIVE OIL shots are a thing again. Remember in the mid- to late-2010s, when people were knocking back all manner of liquids, including apple cider vinegar, juiced ginger, pulverized turmeric, and extra virgin olive oil? Well, social media influencers have brought back at least the last one, with Bryan Johnson doing “evo” shots and Steven Gundry, MD, recommending a daily dose of the stuff. (Also, both those guys sell their own olive oil. Just saying.)

The supposed benefits of shooting olive range from the more generalized “more energy” to more specific ones, like protecting your cardiovascular system from disease.

Influencers usually back up these claims by citing scientific research on the benefits of adding olive oil to your daily diet. But do the benefits extend to doing shots of olive oil?

“Olive oil is great for heart health and contains beneficial polyphenols, but you don't need to take it as a shot. You get the same benefits by just including it in your meals, and it's generally much more enjoyable that way. But if you want to take it as a shot, there's no harm in that. It's just not superior to including olive oil in your diet in regular ways. You know, with food.”

“I'm a fan of California Olive Ranch 100% California Extra Virgin Olive Oil For Everyday. It's high quality, reasonably affordable (for evoo), and widely available. I use it to saute veggies, roast tubers and veggies, and drizzle on various things.”

“I'm a bigger fan of eating a few shots of olive oil with sauteed veggies or I pour it on my food. But a shot is fine I guess. People like to do it in the longevity scene to invoke ancient Greeks doing shots of ‘liquid gold.’”

“I am an affiliate of the Fresh Pressed Olive Oil Club, which I reallly love. I've not had lots of fancy, fresh olive oils from around the world before and now I have. I like seeing and tasting the diversity. I use a 1-liter glass bottle of EVOO, usually Braggs or Whole Food Organic. I try to go through two to three of these a month for our house of four.”

“I wouldn’t suggest shots of olive oil for any reason. All that’s happening here is that you’re adding a concentrated source of calories and fat to your diet. There are no documented benefits of doing this for the average person, and it can cause weight gain if the oil shots are taken in addition to someone’s current diet. This can ultimately cause health problems.”

“I don’t really have a fave oil. I use brands that have a seal of origin in them to ensure their purity.”

“While olive oil is full of beneficial compounds that may support heart health and longevity, shooting it isn't necessary. Heating olive oil may degrade some beneficial polyphenols, but using it as a salad dressing, sauce, or finishing oil for roasted veggies provides the same benefits, plus enhanced absorption of fat-soluble vitamins and carotenoids.”

“Extra-virgin olive oil can have such a range of flavors. I love San Giuliano Coastal Harvest Olive Oil. It's cold-pressed, which helps preserve more nutrients and polyphenols, and has a really nice, peppery flavor. While I don't take shots of it, I regularly use it in dressings and over veggies.”

#5005. Tech, AI Stocks Split After Democratic Sweep: Microsoft Jumps, Tesla and Nvidia Tank

Tech, AI Stocks Split After Democratic Sweep: Microsoft Jumps, Tesla and Nvidia Tank

Technology stocks delivered a mixed performance Wednesday after Democrats swept the U.S. midterm elections, with investors weighing the prospect of tougher artificial intelligence regulation against expectations that a divided government could limit major policy changes.

The Nasdaq-100 climbed 1.14% to 28,500, recovering from an overnight selloff that briefly pushed index futures down more than 2%.

But beneath the broader market rebound, individual technology stocks told sharply different stories.

Microsoft jumped 6.01% to $552.32, while Google parent Alphabet rose 4.63% to $292.99, according to intraday charts reviewed Wednesday.

While software stocks jumped, hardware stocks corrected to the downside. AI chipmaking giant Nvidia fell 2.19% to $202.17, Tesla dropped 3.61% to $384.10, and Intel lost 5.80% to $57.04.

That said, none has yet shown a heavy bearish trend. Even Nvidia, which is going through the most obvious price correction, remains bullish on the longer trend as the prices test a weak support near the $200 price zone.

Bitcoin also recovered strongly, trading around $80,512, up 4.12% in the day, after an initial election-related selloff.

The conflicting moves reflect the uncertainty over what Democratic control of Congress will mean for technology companies that have benefited from the Donald Trump administration's approach to deregulation.

According to Reuters, investors are particularly concerned about greater scrutiny of AI data centers—the hardware camps of the AI industry—following Democratic victories in states such as Texas and Georgia, where large infrastructure projects have become politically contentious.

The AI sector was already facing questions over enormous infrastructure spending and whether those investments would generate adequate returns.

One of Wednesday's biggest losers was Trump Media & Technology Group, the company behind Truth Social.

Shares plunged 14.29% to $5.10, extending a decline that had been underway long before Democrats secured control of Congress.

The stock had already lost approximately 55% of its value year-to-date before Tuesday's vote.

Technical indicators show traders had been bearish on the stock for months. Trump Media has been in a technical pattern that traders refer to as a “death cross” since March 2025, and has lost nearly 90% of its market cap since its 2024 cap. A death cross happens when the average price of the last 200 days becomes greater than that of the last 50 days, suggesting the asset is losing value way too quickly.

The Nasdaq-100's recovery is encouraging for technology bulls, but the index remains well below its October highs above 31,000. The 28,000 area represents an immediate support zone, while a move back above 29,000 would strengthen the short-term recovery.

Bitcoin's recovery above $80,000 offers another indication that risk appetite has not disappeared entirely.

Still, Wednesday's split between technology winners and losers suggests investors are becoming increasingly selective about their exposure to the AI boom—and the political risks surrounding it.

#5002. Bitcoin hits $80.5K as US stocks rally on midterm election results

Bitcoin climbed toward $80,500 as risk assets rallied on Democrats’ midterm election gains and oil prices fell.

Bitcoin (BTC) returned to $80,000 at Wednesday’s Wall Street open as US stocks surged following a Democratic sweep in the midterm elections.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD up by over 4%, having rebounded from its lowest levels in seven weeks.

BTC/USD four-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

US stock futures made swift gains on Wednesday after initially dipping overnight. S&P 500 futures fell as low as 7,490 points, their lowest level since July 31, before reaching 7,690 points, approaching new month-to-date highs.

S&P 500 futures one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#4999. What Is x402? How AI Agents Pay With Crypto

What Is x402? How AI Agents Pay With Crypto

Nobody really built the web with payments in mind. Early HTTP specifications included a status code, 402 "Payment Required", that was reserved for a future system of online payments. Then it sat unused for about 30 years. Payments were slow and expensive, and they needed accounts, card details and middlemen who could reverse charges. None of that works for a small payment made by software.

That gap matters more now because AI agents are starting to browse, call APIs and buy services for their users. An agent can't fill in a checkout form or sign up for a monthly plan for every tool it uses. Cloudflare says more than half of internet traffic is now non-human, and it reports seeing a billion HTTP 402 responses on its network every day.

x402 is an attempt to finally give the internet a native way to pay. This guide covers what it is, how it works, who backs it, and whether there's a token involved.

The 402 "Payment Required" code has been in the HTTP standard since the 1990s. It was set aside "for future use", and x402 is the first big effort to actually use it.

x402 is an open payment standard built on top of HTTP, the protocol that moves data around the web. Coinbase launched it in May 2025 with partners including AWS, Anthropic, Circle and NEAR. It lets APIs, apps and AI agents pay for services in stablecoins as part of an ordinary web request.

x402 is not a blockchain and it is not a coin. It's a set of rules: message formats, headers and a request-and-response flow that any server or client can follow. Payments are mostly made in USDC, Circle's dollar-backed stablecoin, with Coinbase's Ethereum layer-2 network Base as the main settlement chain. The protocol works with any chain, though, and it now supports Solana, Polygon, Arbitrum and others, as well as traditional card payments.

On April 2, 2026, the Linux Foundation announced it was launching the x402 Foundation, with Coinbase contributing the protocol and companies including Microsoft, Google, Visa and Mastercard expressing initial support. The new x402 Foundation became fully operational on July 14, 2026, and now stewards the protocol under vendor-neutral governance.

We are excited to introduce x402–a new open source payments protocol built on HTTP rails.

Let’s break it down: pic.twitter.com/9PNI49NK7A

— Coinbase Developer Platform🛡️ (@CoinbaseDev) May 6, 2025

x402 works through a short handshake inside a normal web request:

Most of the onchain work is done by a facilitator, a service that verifies payments and settles them on the blockchain so sellers don't have to run their own crypto setup. On EVM chains, x402 uses EIP-3009, a standard that lets someone transfer tokens by signing a permission instead of sending a transaction themselves. This means the buyer doesn't need to hold ETH for gas.

x402's main pitch is very small payments made by machines. Settlement on Base takes seconds and costs well under a cent, so it makes sense to pay fractions of a cent for a single API call. There's no sign-up, no API key and no subscription. The agent just pays and gets what it asked for.

Adoption has grown quickly. Coinbase released x402 V2 in December 2025, which added wallet-based identity so agents don't have to pay again on every call, automatic service discovery and support for more chains. In May 2026 it added batch settlement, where agents hand sellers signed vouchers that can be settled in bulk. x402 had processed more than 196 million transactions across 12 chains as of November 1, 2026, settling more than $42 million in total, according to Fireblocks, which cited data from tracker agenteconomy.to. Coinbase says more than 97% of onchain agent transactions in Q2 2026 used x402.

It's worth noting, however, that more than half of x402's lifetime transactions came from a two-month frenzy in late 2025, when traders ran automated scripts to mint and sell PING, a token issued through x402, according to Fireblocks. Activity has since settled at a much lower level: in October 2026, x402 processed 5.1 million transactions worth about $274,000.

No. x402 is a protocol, not a cryptocurrency, and it has no official native token. Payments are made in existing stablecoins, mostly USDC. Speculative "x402" tokens have appeared on various chains, but none of them is issued or backed by Coinbase or the x402 Foundation. Be very wary of any token that claims to be the official x402 coin.

To use x402, an agent or app needs a wallet holding a supported stablecoin on a supported network, usually USDC on Base. USDC is widely available on major exchanges including Coinbase, Kraken and Binance. Coinbase lets users send USDC straight to Base. Developers can also use Coinbase Developer Platform wallets or Circle Wallets to give their agents funds, and set spending limits to keep those agents under control.

The x402 Foundation is growing fast. It had 40 member organizations when it became operational in July 2026, and its member list has since grown to 57, including Visa, Mastercard, American Express, PayPal, Google, Shopify, Circle, Stripe and AWS. Michael Hursta became the foundation's executive director on October 1, 2026.

x402 isn't the only option, though. The Agentic Commerce Protocol, an open standard developed by Stripe and OpenAI, and Visa's Trusted Agent Protocol are competing for a role in agent payments, even though Stripe and Visa are also premier members of the x402 Foundation.

Stripe has also co-developed the Machine Payments Protocol with payments blockchain Tempo. Like x402, MPP uses the HTTP 402 code to let agents pay for services, but adds session-based billing so agents can pay as they go rather than settling every request onchain.

Since June 2026, it has also supported x402 payments. The big open question is whether x402's huge transaction counts turn into steady, real commerce. If they do, the payment code that sat unused for 30 years could end up carrying much of the AI economy's payments.

#4992. Circle’s ARC token sale lifts earnings as lower yields weigh on USDC income

Circle is looking to payments fees and future staking rewards to diversify revenue as its core stablecoin business faces pressure from lower yields and growing competition.

Circle’s expansion beyond stablecoins supplied the biggest boost to its third-quarter earnings, as the company recognized $182 million in revenue from its ARC token presale, helping offset falling income from the reserves backing its flagship stablecoin, USDC.

The company reported diluted earnings per share of $0.84, up from $0.64 a year earlier and double analysts’ expectations of $0.42. Total revenue and reserve income rose 17% to $865 million. Without the ARC token sale, however, revenue would have fallen from a year earlier.

Circle shares were down 0.84% at $74.59 at 4:05 pm UTC on Nov. 4, according to Yahoo Finance.

The results capture a transition for Circle. Its established stablecoin business earns returns on the reserves backing USDC, tying income to interest rates and the amount of the token outstanding. Its newer businesses aim to earn from the payments and financial activity those digital dollars support.

Circle launched its payments network in May 2025 to connect financial institutions settling cross-border payments with stablecoins. Arc, its blockchain for financial applications, went live on public mainnet on Sept. 16, 2026. Its native token, ARC, is intended to support network security and governance as Arc moves toward proof of stake in 2027, while transaction fees are paid in USDC.

That contribution arrived as lower reserve yields outweighed growth in USDC balances. Average circulation increased 8% year over year, but the reserve return fell 61 basis points to 3.5%. Reserve income declined 8% to $657.6 million.

Interest rates were lower than a year earlier, despite a Federal Reserve rate hike in September. The Fed raised its benchmark range by a quarter percentage point to 3.75%–4% in September, although that remained below the 4%–4.25% range set a year earlier. Circle’s reserve return also edged up from 3.48% in the second quarter, but the earnings pressure reflected the lower year-over-year yield.

USDC ended September at $73.9 billion in circulation, while its share of the stablecoin market fell 1.83 percentage points to 27%.

Competition is also expanding. Open Standard’s Open USD was live on Solana by Sept. 30, with Coinbase, Mastercard, Shopify, Stripe and Visa as founding partners. The stablecoin model distributes reserve earnings to partners after a management fee, giving businesses another option for accessing stablecoins and sharing their economics.

Related: ARK buys $17M in Coinbase, Circle shares while trimming Bullish

ARC changed Circle’s earnings picture. Excluding ARC presale revenue, total revenue and reserve income were $683 million, down roughly 8% year over year. Reported adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization rose 85% to $317 million. Without the ARC contribution, adjusted EBITDA was approximately $135 million, down 21% from $171 million a year earlier.

Circle anticipates staking rewards after Arc transitions to proof of stake, which it targets for 2027. That would create another source of revenue, although it remains dependent on the network’s development.

Circle is also beginning to charge for some payments-network activity. Chief Financial Officer Jeremy Fox-Geen said on the earnings call that the company expected material CPN revenue in 2027. CEO Jeremy Allaire described Circle’s strategy as building connected networks, with growth in stablecoins, payments and blockchain applications reinforcing one another.

“This is not just about payments, and it is not just about digital dollars. It is about multiple compounding flywheels on one platform. Every new member, every new builder, and every new asset makes the whole platform more valuable,” Allaire said.

The company is also adjusting its workforce size. The earnings disclosures included a reduction affecting approximately 5% of employees, which Circle said was intended to align its organization with its operating model and strategic priorities.

#4991. Canton Coin Gets SEC Nod as Wall Street Tokenization Heats Up

Canton Coin Gets SEC Nod as Wall Street Tokenization Heats Up

The blockchain behind Wall Street’s newest tokenization service just got a regulatory nod for its native token.

The SEC’s Division of Corporation Finance said in a letter dated Tuesday that it views Canton Coin, the native token of the Canton Network, as a “digital commodity” under the agency’s March interpretation of how securities laws apply to crypto.

Agency staff also said they won’t recommend enforcement action if Canton’s validators and application providers mint the token programmatically without registering it.

The relief comes a week after the Depository Trust & Clearing Corp. launched a tokenization service that runs on Canton and DTCC’s own private network, letting firms convert DTC-held stocks and Treasuries into tokens. Morgan Stanley said Tuesday that it was among the first banks to use the service.

SEC staff pointed to two representations from Canton’s lawyers. The first is that the token derives its value from the network’s programmatic operation and from supply and demand, rather than from the managerial efforts of others.

The second is that the network has no “central party.” Canton Coin launched without an initial coin offering or premine, and tokens were initially earned only by running network infrastructure and applications, according to a statement from Canton. Canton’s request letter was dated Oct. 30, four days before staff responded.

The letter has limits, though, as it reflects only staff’s position on enforcement, not a legal conclusion, and rests on the facts Canton presented, the SEC noted.

“Financial institutions need confidence in both the technology they use and the economic model that supports it,” said Yuval Rooz, CEO of Digital Asset, which created the Canton Network.

Canton said the letter is the first relief of its kind since the SEC published its March interpretive release, which found that most crypto assets aren’t securities. It’s also the first since the agency’s August Regulation Crypto Assets proposal.

The decision fits a broader shift since the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate in September, with the SEC and CFTC taking the lead on crypto rules. SEC Chair Paul Atkins said in July the agency was ready to act if Congress didn’t, and the SEC recently proposed rules on how advisers and funds hold crypto.

Digital Asset raised $355 million earlier this year from Wall Street firms and a sovereign wealth fund.

The added spandex adds stretch for a flexible, flattering fit that moves with you all day. They also come in Petite and Plus sizes, so you can find the perfect fit for your body type. The stretchy elastic waistband helps them stay in place without digging into your skin. Plus, these leggings hug your curves in all the right places, making them oh-so-flattering.

After testing countless leggings (and athletic brands) over the years, I can confidently say Alo leggings are my favorite, especially for shaping and sculpting. Because of their high nylon content, these leggings are super stretchy (in a good way), delivering an ultimate sculpting effect thanks to their medium-compression fit. This tight yet comfortable fit also offers tummy-tucking power and curve-accenting features that look flattering from every angle. Plus, the compression-like feel supports muscles while keeping you warm. The added fleece lining offers both warmth and insulation, making them ideal for workouts or everyday wear in cooler weather. Oh, and did we mention they feel buttery soft on the skin?

Let’s be honest: fleece-lined leggings can sometimes get too warm, especially when you’re working up a sweat. But that’s not the case with these best-selling lululemon leggings. Their brushed fleece interior locks in heat for chilly runs, while the lightweight nylon keeps them breathable so you don’t overheat. Plus, the nylon and Lycra repel water, offering extra protection from rain, sleet, and biting wind—perfect for unpredictable weather.

For runners and outdoor enthusiasts, these leggings hit the sweet spot for warmth, breathability, and performance. The soft fleece keeps you toasty, while the breathable fabric wicks away sweat and prevents that dreaded overheating. With four-way stretch, they move with you, giving you the flexibility you need whether you’re running, hiking, or stretching. They stay securely in place, keeping you comfortable and focused on your workout. Even with the cozy fleece lining, these leggings stay sleek and slim, without that bulky feel. The added Lycra fiber adds stretch and helps them hold their shape, so they don't sag or get baggy, even after multiple washes. Just a heads up: They’re prone to pilling, so wash on cold and hang them to dry to keep them looking fresh.

Beyond Yoga is one of my go-to brands, and these leggings are a perfect example. Let’s start with the fabric—it's soft, lightweight, and breathable, making these pants comfortable all day long. The Pacer Fleece features a smooth exterior and a warm brushed interior for movement in the cold. Another bonus? The center-seam-free design eliminates discomfort and prevents any unwanted lines, like camel toes.

Fleece is a soft, synthetic fabric usually made from a polyester blend, often mixed with spandex, nylon, or cotton. Designed to mimic wool's insulating properties, fleece is lighter, more breathable, and dries faster, making it ideal for staying warm. Plus, fleece wicks away moisture and dries quickly, keeping you cozy even if you’re caught in rain or snow. Think of it like wearing a warm hug on a cold day.

When it comes to fleece leggings, the fabric blend is key. Most leggings are made from a mix of polyester and spandex, which delivers the perfect blend of warmth, stretch, and comfort. We’ll give you a quick breakdown of why these materials are key here:

Polyester traps heat, making it an ideal blend for staying warm without bulk. Compared to natural fibers like cotton, polyester dries quickly. WH editors love wearing polyester-fleece leggings on long walks since they stay warm without making you feel sweaty. It’s super lightweight yet strong, meaning you won’t feel like the material is clinging to your body. Polyester also helps leggings retain their shape over time, so stretching, sagging, and pilling are likely a thing of the past.

Nylon is a popular material in fleece leggings because of its strength, flexibility, and smooth texture. It resists wear and tear, which helps keep leggings looking brand new after every wash. Nylon also offers water resistance, which is key for outdoor activities in light rain or snow.

Spandex (also known as elastane or Lycra) is the go-to fabric for stretch and flexibility. It fits snugly and moves with you, giving you maximum comfort during high-impact activities like yoga, running, and dance. While it’s not as durable on its own, spandex is often blended with other fabrics (like nylon or polyester) to create leggings that are both stretchy and long-lasting.

Here’s the thing: Fleece leggings with thicker fabric may feel more restrictive than traditional leggings, so if you plan to wear them for high-impact activities, opting for a bigger size might be best. Look for four-way stretch fabrics like spandex for flexibility and comfort. If you're between sizes, choose based on your desired fit—size down for a sculpted look (read: tighter) or up for a relaxed, lounge-friendly feel.

Fleece leggings are perfect for workouts, offering warmth and insulation while keeping the stretch and comfort you need for full range of motion. “The leggings should have a good amount of stretch while fitting snugly, and maintaining their shape throughout the day,” says fashion designer Kadeem Alphanso Fyffe. Look for features like a wide, high-rise waistband for a secure fit.

As writers who've tried more than our fair share of leggings, we know what it takes to make a great pair. To earn a place on this list, these fleece leggings had to meet strict criteria. We analyzed everything from fabric quality and warmth to stretch, fit, and breathability. Were they soft and supportive? Did they hold their shape after multiple washes? Could they withstand the elements without pilling or wearing thin? We examined every seam, ensuring each recommendation is a worthy investment for your winter wardrobe and beyond.

We also consulted two style experts, who shared their favorite pairs and provided insight on what to consider when shopping for fleece leggings. They emphasized that materials, stretch, and overall style matter, since they can make or break a pair of leggings (and pants, for that matter).

#4985. It Just Got Easier to Get Peptides in the US. Here's What Could Come Next.

Spoiler: More access doesn’t necessarily mean more safety.

ON WEDNESDAY, THE U.S. Food and Drug Administration announced a new policy that makes six experimental peptides more readily available. According to a document released by the HHS, the government will not penalize specialized pharmacies for making and supplying these popular substances to patients with a prescription. The peptides: BPC-157, epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500.

This does not mean these particular peptides have been approved for use by the FDA. In fact, none of them have gone through the rigorous human testing required for FDA drug approval. And the new policy is not intended to be permanent. Instead, it allows temporary access as the FDA decides on a longer-term plan for the six peptides. It also includes the caveat that compounders report “serious adverse events, and product quality issues to FDA and to the States where the products are compounded,” according to the document.

This topic has been red-hot since July, when the FDA held an advisory committee meeting to consider whether to add these peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply. These substances are already widely used and obtained on the gray market. If people are going to use them, advocates have argued, it would be safest if those substances came from licensed pharmacies and are used with physician oversight.

Critics, on the other hand—including FDA scientists—are concerned that the government may be opening the floodgates to under-studied and potentially harmful drugs. These substances, they note, have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness. And some of these peptides are already being promoted and sold on the basis of scientific claims that experts say are unsupported or questionable.

Here's what could come next, if you're thinking about taking one of these six compounded peptides.

Some parts of the current conversation played out at last month’s PepMed conference in Washington, D.C. A sign pointing guests to the registration table featured a stylized gold peptide overlaid on the white dome of the U.S. Capitol. PepMed’s organizer, the American Academy of Peptide Medicine (AAPM), founded just last year, has already become a major voice in this debate.

In advance of the FDA’s July meeting, AAPM submitted hundreds of pages to a crowded public docket, including information about each of the seven peptides then under review. Several of its advisory board members spoke during the meeting’s public comment sessions. And the group appears to have the ear of the administration. PepMed speakers included the assistant secretary for health, Admiral Brian Christine, and a host of prominent MAHA figures.

The September event opened with an on-the-record media hour, featuring a dozen speakers, including physicians and owners and CEOs of compounding pharmacies and telehealth companies. A recurring theme was the danger of the gray market, where peptide quality can be difficult or impossible to verify and consumers often make treatment decisions without medical oversight—“this is not a victory for patient safety,” said Ricardo Rossello, PhD, a U.S. congressional delegate for Puerto Rico and AAPM advisory board member who co-founded Beijing Prosperous Biopharm, a medical research company.

Many of the speakers also lamented the failure of the U.S. health care system to fully meet the needs of patients, and some of the day’s speakers, including Alex Tatem, MD, an Indiana-based urologist and men’s health specialist with a large YouTube following, presented experimental peptides as part of the solution. “Our established medical system is very much a sick care model,” said Tatem, who serves on the AAPM’s medical advisory board. It’s important to help patients in a health crisis, he told me in an interview. But people want to be proactive about disease prevention. In his view, the peptides under FDA’s consideration can play a role by expanding “the toolkit that’s available to providers.”

At the FDA’s July advisory committee meeting, the agency’s scientists advised against allowing broad access to these peptides, in part because they have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness in the kind of long-term, rigorously designed human studies typically required for drug approval.Even at the conference, some doctors acknowledged the evidence gaps. Abud Bakri, MD, works in California as a physician who specializes in caring for hospitalized patients. He also runs a separate medical practice focused on preventive and longevity medicine, and he serves on the Enhanced Games Independent Medical Commission. His interest in peptides has been long-standing enough to predate his medical career.

Bakri, now 33, has been a powerlifter since he was 17. He explained that he has a genetic condition that may increase his risk for ligament injuries. He first used BPC-157 almost a decade ago, and believes the peptide helped him recover faster, though he acknowledges that this is hard to prove definitively, given the absence of well-designed human studies.

If just a fraction of the rat and Petri dish research holds up in humans, BPC-157 could be an incredibly helpful drug in the ICU, he told me at the conference, during an interview at an outdoor dining area. But ideally, he says, its effectiveness should be proven before it’s used in this setting and potential side effects should be better understood. “If I’m going to have an honest conversation with a patient,” he said, “I need to be able to fully elucidate the risk, which I can’t for some of these compounds.”

After the conference, I spoke by Zoom with Thomas Kremen, MD, a physician at UCLA Health who works for several athletic teams, including the Los Angeles Chargers and Lakers. Kremen and his colleagues recently reviewed the evidence behind six popular experimental peptides. He and his co-authors seemed unimpressed. “It’s probably not a strong stance to be recommending these things,” he told me.

Would it be accurate for physicians to tell their patients that a peptide like BPC-157 heals tissue?

“Maybe if they're talking about a rat in some study,” he said. “But I think if you're making a claim, you better be able to cite the literature that supports that.”

Right now, not only is there a dearth of high-quality clinical evidence that peptides work and are safe, unsupported claims are being made about these compounds.

For instance, among the documents that AAPM submitted to the FDA’s public docket was a white paper claiming that two experimental peptides—epitalon and BPC-157—are endogenous, indicating they are naturally produced by the body. Similar descriptions can be found on websites for telehealth companies and online peptide vendors suggesting their products are synthetic versions of naturally derived peptides.

Epitalon is a lab-made peptide based on an extract that researchers in the Soviet Union obtained from cattle brains. Terry Roberts, PhD, a researcher who studies epitalon at Brunel University of London, said it’s unclear whether humans produce this peptide. As far as he’s aware, no studies have explicitly described its presence in humans.

And BPC-157? The peptide was discovered in gastric juice, said Vince Luca, PhD, a researcher at the Moffitt Cancer Center in Florida. Yet 35 years after its discovery, nobody has found a gene that encodes for it—it could have come from a microbe or from food. It’s not impossible that the body produces BPC-157, said Luca, but he thinks it’s unlikely. He also says that health care professionals “should know enough not to tell [people] that it's an endogenous peptide” when it is very unlikely to be one. And regardless, he continued, “there are plenty of endogenous peptides that, injected at high concentrations, would have a really negative effect on the body.” In other words, being endogenous alone doesn’t mean it’s safe.

(The American Academy of Peptide Medicine’s press team did not respond to queries asking how the group determined that BPC-157 and epitalon are endogenous.)

“My prediction is that those who are already using the gray market will continue to do so,” said Ryan Beattie, director of business development at Adaptog Research & Life Sciences, a UK-based company that sells experimental peptides. “So I think in the initial term, people will use compounding pharmacies in the US. What I then predict will happen is people will then get used to it, get more aware of it—and then switch to the gray market because of the cost.”

He may be right. For this story, we spoke with two peptide users who had been living with chronic pain, and after trying physical therapy as well as other treatment approaches, turned to the Wolverine stack, a combination of BPC-157 and TB-500. Both said they felt the peptides helped them—and neither was sure they’d switch to compounded versions, which are more expensive than the gray market and aren’t covered by insurance. One of them, Charles A., asked, “What are the hoops and what are the costs going to be?”

A third person, who asked to be referred to as James—the name he uses in online communities—tried an oral version of BPC-157 four years ago and said he hasn’t been the same since then. Shortly after taking the experimental compound, he felt anxiety followed by sensory and emotional numbness that still hasn’t gone away. He also developed an autoimmune condition. Online, he found others with some similar experiences. When he looked carefully at the published research, he concluded that it didn’t match the online hype. “I felt very foolish afterwards.”

Some scientists are continuing to investigate these peptides. At Moffitt, Luca is currently studying BPC-157 and he’s keeping an open mind.

Over the next few months, he and his colleagues plan to run a variety of very basic experiments in which they add the peptide to human cells and see how those cells respond. The results could provide additional insight into what, precisely, this molecule is doing in the body—and they may help explain why users have reported everything from striking benefits to lasting harm.

Perhaps one day, BPC-157 will prove to be an effective drug, said Luca. But for now, his advice is to wait on using it and other experimental peptides: “I just worry that people are a little too cavalier in how these are handled.”

Sara Talpos is a Michigan-based journalist covering biomedical research and public health. She’s written for publications including STAT, Science, and Undark, where she’s a contributing editor.

#4983. US Voters Back AI Data Center Restrictions in At Least 29 Communities

US Voters Back AI Data Center Restrictions in At Least 29 Communities

Voters in at least 29 U.S. jurisdictions backed bans or restrictions on data-center development Tuesday, according to The Washington Post, dealing a new setback to the massive infrastructure buildout behind artificial intelligence.

The measures ranged from outright prohibitions to requirements for additional public approval. Some were nonbinding advisory votes, meaning their passage does not automatically stop construction.

Ballotpedia tracked 41 data-center measures on November 3 ballots across 10 states. Twenty-eight were framed so that a yes vote would curb development, while 13 would permit or support projects, meaning rejecting one of the latter could also count as a defeat for developers.

Ohio produced some of the clearest results. All 10 charter measures backed by the grassroots group Conserve Ohio passed, according to the Post, and more than three-quarters of voters in Hubbard, Ohio, supported a ban on data centers.

The negative sentiment is so big that voters in Granville, Trenton, and Sunbury backed restrictions with margins of roughly 78% to 82%.

In Illinois, nearly 80% of voters in Joliet Township rejected an advisory proposal supporting AI data centers. In Janesville, Wisconsin, more than 60% approved a requirement for another public vote before major development at a former General Motors site linked to a proposed data center.

These facilities house the chips and servers used to train and run AI systems. Their appetite for electricity has turned proposed campuses into local fights over utility bills and grid capacity, while opponents also raise concerns about water use, noise and deals struck without public scrutiny.

AI companies are aware of the growing backlash and are already working to counter it. Some are stepping up lobbying efforts, while others are turning to public relations campaigns to reshape public perception. OpenAI, for instance, has sought to rebrand data centers as “compute campuses,” in an effort to distance the facilities from the negative connotations increasingly associated with them.

The votes arrived alongside victories by politicians promising tougher oversight of data centers. Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott in Texas after campaigning for a moratorium on new projects, while Ohio's governor-elect Amy Acton also supports a pause and stricter rules.

Industry leaders have warned that blanket restrictions would put U.S. AI investment at risk. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink said last month that a broad moratorium could have serious economic consequences. "If we have a moratorium of some sort in this country, we will be in a recession," he said.

#4981. Riot shares surge on $28B AI lease as Hut 8 sinks on revenue miss

Bitcoin miner Riot sold BTC to finance its AI expansion, while Hut 8 reported falling revenue and rising costs before its major data center leases generate income.

Shares of Bitcoin miner Riot Platforms surged more than 13% Wednesday after the company announced a $28 billion AI data center lease, its largest agreement to date as it accelerates its expansion into artificial intelligence infrastructure.

The 15-year agreement covers 910 megawatts at its Corsicana, Texas, facility, bringing its total future contracted data center revenue to approximately $38 billion across three leases signed this year. The agreement represents a major step in Riot’s AI infrastructure strategy as it moves away from relying primarily on Bitcoin mining revenue.

“A year ago, our cash flow depended on the price of Bitcoin, and now, at full deployment of currently executed deals, nearly all of our expected cash flow will be generated from long-term leases with leading players in the AI ecosystem,” CEO Jason Les said during Wednesday’s earnings call.

According to Riot’s third-quarter earnings release, Riot will provide land and power infrastructure originally developed for Bitcoin mining, while the AI tenant, identified as Anthropic in the company’s quarterly filing, will own and operate the computing equipment and cover operating expenses.

Riot estimates the project’s total capital costs at $5.5 billion, financed primarily through debt backed by lease payments, although financing has not yet been secured. The first phase is expected to be delivered in September 2027, with rental payments beginning within 60 days of delivery.

Riot also reported third-quarter revenue of $181.4 million, up just 1% year over year, and sold 10,599 BTC during the quarter to help finance its data center expansion. During Wednesday’s earnings call, CEO Jason Les said Bitcoin mining at Corsicana could begin winding down in summer 2027.

Related: Bitcoin miners face widening AI financing costs as lenders scrutinize deals

Meanwhile, shares of fellow Bitcoin miner Hut 8 plunged more than 22% at one point on Wednesday after the company reported weaker-than-expected quarterly revenue amid its transition toward AI infrastructure.

Third-quarter revenue fell 15% to $71.1 million, missing analysts’ estimates of $75.8 million, according to MarketBeat data. During Wednesday’s earnings call, CFO Sean Glennan attributed the decline partly to asset sales and lower revenue per Bitcoin mined, while cash administrative expenses also rose year over year.

“The legacy revenue base is no longer the main growth driver, while the larger contracted infrastructure revenues remain tied to delivery milestones,” Glennan said.

CEO Asher Genoot also pointed to the complexity and expense of traditional AI data center construction as the company looks to develop projects on a larger scale.

“Many of today’s data centers resemble NASA rocket programs—remarkable engineering, but complex, expensive, and slow to deliver,” Genoot said.

According to the company’s third-quarter earnings presentation, Hut 8 has already secured $7.5 billion in project bonds to fully finance two major AI data center developments, although neither has begun generating lease revenue.
