#4940. 10 Best Airbnbs in London With Brilliant Design (2026)

If London excels at anything, it’s lodgings with soul—from the silver-service glamour of Claridge’s to the shiny new scenes of At Sloane. But these days, it’s the city’s next generation of stays—creative, design-led rentals that might include a sweet stay tucked into a mews, a contemporary warehouse with exposed brick, or a period town house on a leafy street—that capture the true spirit of living like a local. In a place where nightly rates can soar well into four figures, claiming your own corner of the city feels more like the move. Here are the London Airbnbs where you can carry on like a local—homes that are made for sinking back into after a morning finding the best coffee or after a late night in Soho.

Perks: Sauna, sound system, fully-equipped kitchen, curated book selection, washer and dryer, centrally located near St. Pancras train station

Tucked away on a quiet corner in Islington, this three-story, Japanese-inspired stay is filled with wabi-sabi design moments you’ll want to steal for your own space. Guided by a minimal color palette, light-toned wood weaves cohesion throughout the interiors, which are outfitted with architectural details and decor accents. On cloudless days, sunlight bathes the upper levels, giving the wood flourishes a warm glow. The main living area trades a traditional sofa for tatami mats, where you can perform traditional tea ceremonies or simply enjoy a morning matcha. In the upstairs bedroom, camouflaged closets and drawers surround a grounded bed frame, while a freestanding tub placed right in front of the sleeping arrangements invites you for a relaxing soak.

The downstairs kitchen serves a helping of maximalism with its striking red marble center island and coordinating stove backsplash. Just steps away from the refrigerator, a hidden walk-in shower, sauna, and spare bathroom are discreetly tucked away. The owners describe this home as a “domestic gallery,” but its beauty doesn’t make it any less liveable. “Every detail is thoughtfully considered,” says shopping editor Audrey Lee. “A Space Residence reimagines mundane fixtures into modern design moments that make you feel like you’re living in art. Relaxing in the tub with a good book, enjoying a slow morning watching television in the kitchen, or taking a steam in the sauna are just a few ways the days will quickly pass in this lovely home away from home.” From $549 per night.

Perks: Garden views, elevator, luggage drop-off allowed, proximity to Notting Hill landmarks

This hotel, available to book on Airbnb, is situated in one of London’s most scenic neighborhoods. Surrounded by Notting Hill’s pastel town houses, the Portobello Hotel’s interiors are their own draw, sporting vibrant colors and unexpected textural pairings. The property has a cohesive design thread in its use of maximalism, though no two guest rooms look quite alike, providing a sense of unique eclecticism. Each room features elaborate headboards, lush textiles, and curated wallpapers, among other design elements that will greet you after a long day spent perusing Portobello Road Market’s endless stalls or snapping a picture with the famed Notting Hill (1999) door. With buzzy restaurants like Core by Clare Smyth and Eel Bar Sushi near the hotel, it’s quite easy to spend the entire day nearby, without ever leaving the neighborhood. From $456 per night.

Perks: Portable fans, board games, washer and dryer, security system, pet-friendly, housekeeping available for a fee

Not only is this three-bedroom duplex in Holland Park a centrally located, elegant stay, but it’s also an architectural landmark. Nestled in London’s Royal Crescent, this apartment occupies space in a Robert Cantwell–designed Victorian building that dates back to 1839. The structure is a Grade II listed building, meaning it carries historical significance in original detailing, like a sweeping stucco façade, grand porticoed entrances, and tall sash windows. Contrastingly, the duplex’s interiors couldn’t be more modern, with skylight windows, sleek kitchen appliances, and poured concrete floors that add a brutalist touch. King or double-sized mattresses in each of the minimally designed bedrooms provide the perfect respite after long days spent visiting London sights like the nearby Japanese-inspired Kyoto Garden and Fukushima Garden, as well as Hyde Park and Kensington Palace just a short walk east. From $721 per night.

Perks: Underfloor heating, gallery-like, architecturally designed

Perks: Private roof terrace, kitchen pantry, playroom/yoga studio, across from Hyde Park

Spread across four beautiful Georgian floors, this charming town house is perfect for families needing extra space to spread out. Inside, interiors marry beauty and coziness—think a charming living room for long evenings in front of the fire, a sophisticated kitchen for big breakfast spreads and shared cooking, and a deep soaking tub for relaxing in. Upstairs, a playroom easily converts into a yoga space, while the roof terrace offers a sweet slice of quiet in the city. You’re just steps from Hyde Park (catch conversations at Speaker’s Corner), Mayfair, and Bond Street. From $2,108 per night.

Perks: Natural light, near Ladbroke Grove station, garden-facing bedroom

This one-bed flat is only a four-minute walk to Portobello Road and just a three-minute walk to the nearest tube station. Ideal for living out a Richard Curtis film, Notting Hill is one of London’s most beautiful corners. Inside, there’s a large bedroom towards the back of the building, which keeps things quiet, but all the natural light will make sitting in the living room the place to be, plus it shines a shadow on the warm woods and textured accents. The table sits six as well, so it’s ideal for hosting. From $523 per night.

Perks: Air-conditioning, original panelling, two bathrooms

Step out the front door of this Georgian home and one way you’ll find the busy scenes of Seven Dials Market, but head the other way and you’ll end up in Covent Garden’s Theatreland, where some of the city’s best sites (including the Royal Opera House) and restaurants have found their footing. Back at base, you’ll find original wood-paneled walls, vaulted living areas, and colorful decorative touches inside. The three bedrooms are fitted with AC and lovely linens, and the open-plan top-floor kitchen meets living room is the perfect place to rest the legs after a day out on the feet. From $1,369 per night.

A six-bedroom Regency town house in the middle of Marylebone, the space consists of six decked-out bedrooms designed by Russel Sage Studio (four posters, lots of silks, velvets and patterns), plus, if you’re after a total takeover, you can add on a full team to support, including a private chef and staff who will be there for any need or drink creation. It’s wonderfully spacious, discreet, and, despite its age, it’s got plenty of modern touches. From $7,960 per night.

Situated between London Fields and Victoria Park, this loft is totally cool and fitting of its east London location. Exposed brick, white-washed floors, and generous glazing allow light to flood in. And the furnishings lean eclectic—midcentury, vintage, art-forward—with a living-meets-dining-room layout that accommodates group gatherings and lounging. When you head outside, Mare Street’s best wine bars and bakeries are just a quick walk away, so it’s ideal for guests who want to experience what this part of the city is really all about. From $435 per night.

Perks: High ceilings, two en suite bedrooms, Sonos sound system, concierge services by Stay & Escape

Right in the heart of charming Clerkenwell, this open-plan loft offers a dose of London cool behind the gates of a private courtyard. Inside, exposed brick walls lined with art, designer furniture, and 22-foot vaulted ceilings keep things spacious. There’s warmth here too—soft Egyptian cotton sheets, fluffy robes, and all the perks of a hotel without actually being one. Every detail feels considered, from the sleek kitchen to the built-in Sonos system. Managed by Stay & Escape, it’s a home that blends high-level polish with soulful, lived-in comfort. We also love how near it is to Exmouth Market, one of London’s best food-lined streets. From $2,249 per night.

#4934. Kia’s EV6 GT beat out the Tesla Model Y and BYD Sealion 7 in a German EV comparison test

Kia’s electric sports car beat out the Tesla Model Y Performance and BYD’s Sealion 7 in a recent comparison test by the German magazine Auto Motor und Sport, ranking first overall.

It wasn’t a German brand or a Chinese one. It wasn’t even the Tesla Model Y Performance. Kia’s electric sports car, the EV6 GT, took first place in a recent high-performance EV test in Germany, the heart of Europe.

The comparison test, held by German auto magazine Auto Motor und Sport, evaluated five high-performance EVs across seven categories: body, safety, comfort and convenience, powertrain, driving performance, environmental friendliness, and running costs.

The EV6 GT ranked first overall with 585 points, 9 points ahead of the Tesla Model Y Performance, which took second with 576 points.

The Zeekr 7GT ranked third with 569 points, followed by the Polestar 4 Coupe Dual Motor with 531 points, and the BYD Sealion 7 AWD Excellence, which ranked fifth with 508 points.

Kia’s electric sports car took the top spot in three of the seven categories: safety, powertrain, and driving performance. In the safety category, the EV6 GT scored highest for its superior braking and high-speed stability.

The EV6 GT earned praise in the powertrain category, boasting up to 650 horsepower and 770 Nm of torque from its dual-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain.

In the driving performance category, Kia’s electric sports car scored 20 points higher (96 points) than the Tesla Model Y Performance in second (76 points).

According to Auto Motor und Sport, the Kia EV6 GT took first place overall in the comparison test due to its “outstanding driving performance.”

Performance is one thing, but it also stands out with fun features, including Virtual Gear Shift. Borrowed from the Hyundai IONIQ 5 N, the feature simulates the feeling and sounds of a six-speed paddle-operated transmission.

Kia overhauled the interior for the newest model with its new connected car Navigation Cockpit (ccNC) infotainment system.

The new system includes dual 12.3″ navigation and driver display screens in a curved panoramic setup.

Kia’s electric sports car can sprint from 0 to 100 km/h (0 to 62 mph) in 3.5 seconds. It uses a new 84 kWh battery, providing a WLTP range of up to 279 miles.

With 800V fast-charging capabilities, the Kia EV6 GT can recharge from 10% to 80% in as little as 18 minutes.

In Germany, the new Kia EV6 GT starts at 69,990 euros ($78,000). The Tesla Model Y Performance, on the other hand, starts at 63,970 euros in Germany. It offers a WLTP range of up to 360 miles (580 km) and accelerates from 0 to 100 km/h (0 to 62 mph) in 3.5 seconds.

Which high-performance EV would you drive? The Kia EV6 GT or the Tesla Model Y Performance? Let us know in the comments.

Unfortunately, Kia said it was discontinuing the EV6 GT in the US “until further notice” earlier this year.

#4932. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Bitcoin climbed above $80,500 as US stocks rallied following the midterm elections. Democrats won control of Congress, raising doubts about the future of the CLARITY bill, while ZachXBT identified French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a crypto theft exceeding $133 million.

Bitcoin (BTC) returned to $80,000 at Wednesday’s Wall Street open as US stocks surged following a Democratic sweep in the midterm elections.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD up by over 4%, having rebounded from its lowest levels in seven weeks.

Data from CoinGlass showed the price dip liquidating late BTC long positions below $76,000, while a wall of asks kept upside contained below $78,000. Total crypto long liquidations over the past 24 hours stood at $288 million.

US stock futures made swift gains on Wednesday after initially dipping overnight. S&P 500 futures fell as low as 7,490 points, their lowest level since July 31, before reaching 7,690 points, approaching new month-to-date highs.

In comments sent to Cointelegraph, James Butterfill, head of research at crypto asset manager CoinShares, saw the potential for a wider Bitcoin “relief rally” as election uncertainty faded.

Democrats have won control of both chambers of the US Congress in the midterm elections, giving the party greater influence over crypto legislation and oversight of President Donald Trump’s administration.

At the time of writing, Democrats had secured 228 House seats to Republicans’ 139, with 68 races still uncalled, according to the Associated Press. The Senate tally stood at 54 seats for Democrats and 44 for Republicans.

The result could derail the CLARITY crypto market structure bill, according to Stefan Muehlbauer, head of US government affairs at CertiK.

“The immediate read on this election result is that CLARITY bill is likely dead,” he said in comments shared with Cointelegraph, referring to both the upcoming lame-duck session and the next Congress.

Muehlbauer said he expected Sen. Elizabeth Warren and Rep. Maxine Waters, both outspoken crypto critics, to chair the Senate Banking Committee and House Financial Services Committee, respectively. Democratic control could also bring greater congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked digital asset businesses.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has identified French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a crypto theft exceeding $133 million, warning that total losses could be higher.

On Wednesday, the pseudonymous crypto sleuth said the institution, which he described as having $235 billion in assets, was allegedly exploited over multiple days. He also suggested that additional losses may have occurred through over-the-counter (OTC) transactions that were not included in earlier estimates.

“I suspect the amount stolen is larger when accounting for OTC activity,” ZachXBT said.

The bank’s alleged involvement has not been independently verified, and ZachXBT has not disclosed how the attackers gained access to the funds.

The disclosure follows ZachXBT’s initial report on Monday, when he identified over $106 million in suspicious withdrawals from centralized exchanges.

On Tuesday, he raised the estimated losses to over $133 million after discovering another $27 million in withdrawals, some of which occurred after his initial warning.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4931. The 25 Best Places to Live in the US (2026-2027)

Each year U.S. News & World Report releases a list of the best places to live in the US. While typically these top-rated locales include a number of big players, like Miami and New York, in the most recent list, suburbs reign supreme and major metropolises are notably absent. To determine the best cities in the country, U.S. News considers a multitude of factors, including quality of life, the local job market, value for your money, and the place’s desirability. And while we agree that all these factors are important in determining where to live in America—which is also ranked on AD’s list of best countries to live in—we’d also throw access to art and design into the mix. That’s why we used the U.S. News list as a jumping-off point—zeroing in on the best places for design lovers among them.

Before you read on, a little more about the methodology:

Carmel, Indiana, earned the No. 1 spot on the U.S. News list thanks in large part to a healthy job market. In this suburb about 20 miles north of Indianapolis, unemployment is lower than the national average, and the median household income is higher: $144,615, compared with $83,181. Plus, Carmel packs on the Midwestern charm, with friendly residents, a walkable downtown, and safe neighborhoods. The schools are excellent here as well. Carmel High School landed on the U.S. News Best High Schools list. And though homes in Carmel are pricier than the national average ($477,625 compared with $359,870), you get plenty for the money.

U.S. News determines quality of life based on how satisfied residents are with their daily lives, considering factors such as crime rates, quality of education, quality and availability of health care, and commute times. Tamarac, Florida, ranked No. 1 for quality of life, with Palo Alto, California; Doral, Florida; and Bethesda, Maryland (included on our list below), right behind it. For those who want quality of life outside of the US, consider our list of the best countries for Americans to move to.

On a separate U.S. News list of The Most Affordable Places to Live in the US, Eagle Pass, Texas, took top honors. Broken Arrow, Oklahoma, landed in the top 20 (at spot 12) and is included on our list below. But within the Best Places to Live list, U.S. News examined each city’s median annual household income compared with home prices in the area. And as you’ll see below, good design and affordable real estate aren’t mutually exclusive. Average housing costs in Ankeny, Iowa; Pearland, Texas; Broken Arrow, Oklahoma; Sioux Falls, South Dakota; El Paso, Texas; Appleton, Wisconsin; Farmington Hills, Michigan; Oklahoma City, Oklahoma; and Cedar Rapids, Iowa, are below the national average, and all of these places have landed on our design-centric list.

We chose our top 25 from the U.S. News list based on access to museums, art, cultural institutions, and top-notch architecture. We also considered which cities best fuel creative energy and make the best places to live for designers, artists, and innovators.

Indiana is a highly celebrated state, claiming the first two spots on the U.S. News list with Carmel and Fishers. Texas also makes a number of appearances in the top 10, thanks to Flower Mound, Leander, Frisco, and Sugar Land. But in a separate U.S. News list of the Best States, the top honor goes to Utah, which earns high marks for its fiscal stability, infrastructure, and excellent education.

The Carmel Arts and Design District is at the heart of the suburb.

Anyone who regularly reads best-places-to-live lists will instantly recognize Carmel—this Indianapolis suburb is always a favorite. It has superb schools, low crime, and a thriving job market. It also has a buzzing Arts and Design District, with galleries, studios, locally owned shops, and public art and sculptures. Plus, the district is a major hub on the Indiana Glass Trail, a collection of artists and studios dedicated to the region’s rich history in glassmaking that stretches across eight counties in the Hoosier State.

A train station in Fishers that was later turned into a pedestrian walkway

Coming in just behind Carmel, Fishers ranks second for places to live in Indiana and second in the national ranking, according to U.S. News. And back in 2017, Money Magazine named it the best place to live in the country. Located about 30 minutes north of Indianapolis, Fishers consistently earns accolades for its safety, top-notch education system, and excellent outdoor attractions, including 100 miles of nature trails, 24 parks, and even an outdoor music venue—the Nickel Plate District Amphitheater. The city also packs a punch in terms of cultural offerings, most notably through The Fishers Art Center, where locals can take pottery, painting, and drawing classes, and explore contemporary art exhibitions.

As the name suggests, Flower Mound is rich in natural beauty, with nearly 1,000 acres of parkland; over 75 miles of trails for hiking, biking, and horseback riding; as well as proximity to two lakes: Lake Grapevine and Lake Lewisville. The town gets its name from the wildflowers—including Texas bluebonnets, pansies, and wild foxglove—that blanket its elevated town center. Flower Mound also has top-tier schools and easy access to major metropolitan areas. Dallas is just 28 miles southeast of town, and Fort Worth is 33 miles southwest. All of the above, plus high ranks in health care and a strong economy, helped Flower Mound earn the No. 1 spot on Livability’s list of Best Places to Live in 2025.

Ankeny is a fast-growing suburb of Des Moines.

The top spot to live in the Hawkeye State, according to U.S. News, is Ankeny, a suburb of Des Moines that began as a farming community—it’s even home to a 455-acre John Deere factory that’s been in operation since 1947. But these days, there’s more to Ankeny than just soybeans, corn, and tractors. The rapidly growing suburb has its fair share of cultural attractions too, including museums such as the Ankeny Area Historical Society Museum, the Iowa Aviation Heritage Museum, and the Ankeny Art Center. Both kids and adults can take classes in pottery, drawing, watercolors, and acrylics at the art center. That is, of course, when they’re not busy cycling the town’s 25-mile High Trestle Trail, which is an art attraction in itself. At night, Blue LED fixtures light up a bridge on the trail, creating a mesmerizing portal-like display. The glowing bridge was made possible by a public art grant, illustrating Ankeny’s commitment to culture in addition to first-rate schools, public parks, and a high standard of living.

Johns Creek, Georgia, is a suburb of Atlanta.

This Atlanta suburb landed in the top five on the U.S. News list (and in the top spot in last year’s ranking) thanks in large part to a healthy job market and a median household income of $163,653 (compared with the national median household income of $79,466). Additionally, the average commute time in Johns Creek is only 26 minutes. That proximity to the city also means access to Atlanta museums such as the Museum of Contemporary Art of Georgia and the High Museum of Art—the Southeast’s largest museum for visual art and features architecture by both Richard Meier and Renzo Piano. In Johns Creek itself, The Art Center is home to exhibits, classes, and workshops. Plus, public art—including various murals and sculptures—can be found in parks and along pedestrian trails around town. The town also has its own orchestra, the Johns Creek Symphony Orchestra.

Hoover Metropolitan Stadium in Hoover, Alabama

Alabama’s top place to live earns high marks for its affordability, with a lower cost of living compared with other similarly sized metro areas. The town, just 20 minutes south of downtown Birmingham, also has a great appreciation for preserving its architecture and parkland. In 1997, the city purchased the historic Aldridge estate, turning it into the Aldridge Gardens, a botanical garden that’s open to the public and offers yoga classes, art exhibits, and group tours of its remarkable hydrangea beds. The city also bestowed a Hoover Historical Society Marker upon its Park Avenue Historical District, a neighborhood with architecture that spans from the late 19th century to just after World War II. The district is home to the 1923 Bluff Park Elementary School, the 1889 Overseer’s House, and the 1910 Hale-Joseph House.

Like Hoover, Alabama, Rochester Hills has a low cost of living when you compare housing costs (an average of $388,231 vs. a national average of $359,870) and the median household income ($119,938 vs $83,181 nationally). This family-friendly town, located about 25 miles north of downtown Detroit, is also celebrated for its low crime rates, top-notch schools, and accessible health care—Henry Ford Rochester Hospital is here. The town also offers plenty to inspire art and design fans, including galleries like Rochester Fine Art Market, Subjectively Objective, and the Oakland University Art Gallery, which is in the neighboring city of Rochester.

This Austin suburb offers proximity to the state capital at a more affordable price. Here, the average housing cost is $495,712, compared with $581,402 in Austin, which is only a 30- to 45-minute drive away. Plus, the CapMetro Rail gives commuters a direct route to downtown on public transportation. Design-wise, Leander offers Texas Hill Country charm, more modern architecture, and even a master-planned community of midcentury-inspired homes. Starlight Village, where construction began about 10 years ago, is a community of homes that look like they could’ve been plucked straight from 1950s Palm Springs—butterfly roofs, orange doors, wood-paneled walls and all.

Located within North Carolina’s Research Triangle—the region that quite literally forms a triangle between Raleigh, Durham, and Chapel Hill, and their research-heavy universities (Duke, UNC-Chapel Hill, and NC State)—Cary is a desirable home for anyone interested in science and tech. The city is also ripe for residents wanting to live somewhere that embraces progressive urban design and planning. The town has even adopted a long-term plan called “Imagine Cary,” a comprehensive vision to guide growth through 2040 and create a sustainable urban-suburban lifestyle. Areas of focus under the plan include transportation, housing, shopping, and environmental initiatives. In addition to looking toward the future, the people of Cary actively preserve the past through notable restoration projects including the Page-Walker Arts & History Center, a former 19th-century hotel that’s now a hub for the arts, and the Nancy Jones House, a residence that dates back to 1803.

The Kresge Foundation headquarters in Troy, Michigan

If it was good enough for legendary architect Minoru Yamasaki, who designed the original World Trade Center, the city of Troy deserves a spot on our list. Yamasaki and his family lived in Troy for 25 years, and he operated his architecture firm, Yamasaki and Associates, out of the Detroit suburb. Locally, his firm designed their office building, the Columbia Center, and the Temple Beth El, a synagogue in nearby Bloomfield Hills. Beyond its architectural history, Troy is celebrated for its strong economy (global consumer intelligence company JD Power is headquartered here), excellent education system, and outdoor recreational offerings—the town has more than 1,000 acres of parkland.

Buffalo Creek Forest Preserve in Buffalo Grove, Illinois

The No. 1 place to live in the Land of Lincoln offers all the benefits of the ’burbs—great schools, low crime rates, and plenty of green space—with proximity to one of the country’s top cities for architecture: Chicago. Buffalo Grove is only about 30 miles northwest of the Windy City, giving its residents easy access to architectural marvels like the Frank Lloyd Wright–designed Robie House and the Wrigley Building, as well as world-class institutions such as the Art Institute of Chicago and the National Museum of Mexican Art. In Buffalo Grove itself, The Raupp Museum tells the history of the city, including an exhibit about Potawatomi life in the 1830s and a replica of an early-20th-century general store.

The Delores Fenwick Nature Center in Pearland, Texas

Affordable housing (the median housing cost is $319,753 compared to a national median of $370,489) and a low unemployment rate (3.6% compared to a national average of 4.5%) helped Pearland nab a spot in the top five on the U.S. News list. The city of 123,986 earns a place on our list because of its dedication to creative living. In 2024, it rolled out a Cultural Arts Master Plan aimed at fostering more cultural events and activities, developing a community arts and cultural center, providing more opportunities for children to participate in the arts, and creating a more vibrant downtown. Add to that, Pearland is only minutes from Houston, home to an Arts District with over 300 studio spaces for creatives of all kinds.

We like Broken Arrow—the fast-growing Tulsa suburb with a history tied to the Muscogee Creek Nation—for its buzzy arts and entertainment center, the Rose District (the name is a nod to the rosebushes that dotted the area’s original landscaping). The district is home to multiple art galleries, a performing arts center, a pavilion for a weekly farmers market, and The Museum Broken Arrow. A hub for local history, the museum gives visitors the chance to experience the town’s Main Street as it would have looked in the early 20th century, plus step inside a Civil War–era cabin. Meanwhile, U.S. News appreciates Broken Arrow for its job market, which is better than the national average, and its affordable housing—the median home value here is $251,667.

Lake Sammamish is a highlight of the Seattle suburb.

The ’burbs often get a bad rap for being nothing more than a series of strip malls, but that couldn’t be further from the truth in Sammamish—one of Seattle’s most desirable suburbs. Located about 30 minutes east of the city, Sammamish offers the best of both worlds: access to the metropolis’s tech-driven job market and jaw-dropping natural beauty. Lush forests, tranquil lakes, and the peaks of the Cascade Mountain Range are all baked into the scenery here. As such, boating, fishing, hiking, and cycling are popular activities among residents, as are visits to nearby Lake Sammamish State Park. Add to that, the city is celebrated for its excellent public schools, low crime rate, quality health care facilities, and culturally diverse community.

Called the “Technology City of the South,” Alpharetta is a hub for major corporations like ADP, Bakkt, and Aptean, and offers plenty of employment opportunities—some even say it has twice as many jobs as residents. The average household income is also higher here: $153,928 compared with $83,181 nationally. On the design front, Alpharetta is celebrated for its diverse residential styles, including Greek Revival, Queen Anne, and Craftsman homes in its historic downtown, as well as French country and European-inspired homes around Lake Windward.

Arlington Heights is located just northwest of Chicago.

Rumors abound that this northwest Windy City suburb might be the future home of the Chicago Bears’ stadium, a major perk for the area’s football fans. Other advantages of living in this city (technically an incorporated village) with a population of roughly 75,000 include access to public transportation (it’s only about 30 minutes to downtown Chicago by train), excellent public schools, a median housing cost that’s only slightly higher than the national average, and a super charming, walkable downtown. Arlington Heights is also home to the Metropolis Performing Arts Centre, where locals can take improv or acting classes as well as catch a show. And of course, with easy access to Chicago, fans of the performing arts are only minutes from the Chicago Theatre, the Auditorium Theatre, and the Lyric Opera House.

Like many of the top cities on this year’s U.S. News list, Bethesda offers the comforts of suburban living with access to a major city—in this case, Washington, DC. But that doesn’t mean locals have to commute for culture. Bethesda has plenty going on in terms of arts and entertainment. For music lovers, there’s the Strathmore, a 1,976-seat theater that regularly hosts the National Philharmonic Orchestra; and the Bethesda Theater, home to concerts as well as comedy shows. Meanwhile theater enthusiasts flock to Round House, and fans of the visual arts find plenty of eye candy in the city’s Arts & Entertainment District, with its galleries and public art.

Outdoor recreation is a strong attraction in Sioux Falls.

Catch the Big Sioux River cascading over pink quartzite rock at the 123-acre Falls Park; explore the world’s largest annual exhibit of public sculptures at SculptureWalk; and hit the powder at Great Bear Ski Valley—it’s all part of the lifestyle in Sioux Falls, South Dakota’s largest city. Living here also means affordable housing—with the median home cost at $315,767, compared to the national average of $370,489—plus no state income tax. The city’s low unemployment rate and excellent public safety are other reasons Sioux City landed in the top 50 on the U.S. News list. But, of course, the real draw here is the area’s natural beauty. In addition to the waterfalls mentioned above, the city is home to a 45-mile system of biking trails that connects various parks; this includes a 19-mile loop along the Big Sioux River.

Round Rock is within easy grasp of Austin but also boasts its own thriving cultural scene.

For those itching to live in Austin (ranked #164 on the U.S. News list) but not thrilled about the capital city’s high costs, Round Rock may be the place to be. Only about 30 minutes north of Austin, Round Rock has a median home value of $446,967, compared to $581,104 in the big city, according to U.S. News. But planting roots in this suburb doesn’t mean you have to totally sacrifice culture. Round Rock has a spirited arts scene, including public murals and sculptures in its downtown, as well as a brand-new creative hub called the City Arts Center. Opened in January 2026, the center is located in downtown’s historic Griffith Building, providing 44,000 square feet and two-story-high cathedral ceilings for art exhibitions, film screenings, and live music.

An aerial view of El Paso, Texas, and Juarez, Mexico

Affordable housing isn’t El Paso’s only draw, but it’s a big one. The average house costs $196,914 here, compared with a national average of $359,870. And for roughly 45% less, you get nearly 300 days of sunshine and the largest state park in an urban setting. Franklin Mountains State Park is almost 27,000 acres of desert terrain, including over 100 miles of trails for hiking and biking. Architecturally, you’ll find everything from adobe to Art Deco styles. The city’s Mission of Guadalupe dates all the way back to the 1650s, and the Socorro Mission was built in 1840. Prolific architect Henry Trost rolled into town in 1903, and he and his firm would eventually design more than 300 buildings in El Paso, adding Prairie, Spanish Colonial, Art Deco, Gothic, and Byzantine styles to the area.

For a luxury apartment in a historic high-rise, you might think you have to head to a major city, like New York or Chicago. But Appleton, about 30 miles southwest of Green Bay, offers architecturally unique apartment living. Its neo-Gothic Zuelke Building, constructed in 1931 and the city’s first high-rise, has been converted into luxury apartments, with the building’s original brass elevators and pink marble floors restored. Nearby, the Historic Fox River Mills Apartments are spread across three restored paper mills. The brick buildings, all of which are on the National Register of Historic Places, date from 1883 through 1915. And it’s not just the apartments that are appealing to design lovers—downtown Appleton is a hub for creativity. In addition to live music, the Trout Museum of Art, and the Fox Cities Performing Arts Center, the area is home to Creative Downtown Appleton, Inc., an organization that actively funds public art and urban design improvements.

Snowcapped Mount Rainier in Bellevue, Washington

Located directly across Lake Washington from Seattle, Bellevue earns high praise for its booming economy. Major corporations, including Microsoft, Amazon, T-Mobile, and Expedia Group, have offices here, and the average household income is $172,076, compared with a national average of $83,181. Of course it’s a good thing that wages run higher here, as the average home value is a staggering $1,304,230. But if you can swing the high cost, you’ll also get top-tier schools, an exceptionally safe and clean city, and extraordinary outdoor experiences. Take, for example, Bellevue Downtown Park, a 21-acre oasis in the heart of the city that features a 240-foot-wide waterfall and views of Mount Rainier in the distance.

Farmington Hills is about 20 miles northwest of downtown Detroit.

This Detroit suburb has Michigan’s largest dedicated municipal Cultural Arts Division, which coordinates year-round programs, including classes, concerts, and exhibitions. Farmington Hills is also home to The Hawk—an award-winning 270,000-square-foot recreation and arts venue that includes the Hawk Theatre, where Grammy-celebrated musicians have performed; several 2D and 3D art studios; gallery spaces that showcase local, regional, and national talent; and the Hawk Makerspace, an immersive shared creative and technical studio. The city’s Cultural Art Division also plans and juries Art on the Grand, an annual fair held in neighboring Farmington each June. The event features paintings, ceramics, and photography for sale, plus art demos and live music.

Though the median household income here is just under the national average, both the unemployment rate and average housing cost are also below the national average. In terms of cultural appeal, Oklahoma’s largest city and its capital has a number of museums, including the Oklahoma City Museum of Art and performing arts venues that host ballets, operas, and musical theater productions. Plus, OKC’s Paseo Arts District, a neighborhood just north of downtown, has 80 working artists and 20 studios that take part in an art walk the first Friday of every month, sharing their latest collections.

In the Hawkeye State, Cedar Rapids has a reputation as an artistic hub. In the 1920s and ’30s, American Regionalist painter Grant Wood, best known for his work American Gothic, lived here. Residents can enjoy his creations at various locations around town, including at the Cedar Rapids Museum of Art, home to the world’s largest collection of work by Wood; at the Veterans Memorial Building, which has a Wood-designed 24-foot-tall stained-glass window; and at the Grant Wood Studio, the artist’s former home and workspace, now owned and operated by the Cedar Rapids Museum of Art. Add to that, a number of Wood’s paintings can be found hanging in the town’s elementary and high schools, giving local kids regular access to celebrated art.

Job opportunities in tech, education, and health care abound in Austin, as do activities for the design-loving crowd. Museums in town include The Contemporary Austin, the Blanton Museum of Art, and the Mexic-Arte Museum. The last in that list is located in Austin’s bustling Warehouse District, where old warehouses have been ingeniously redesigned to host restaurants, bars, and shops. Twice a year, the arts nonprofit Big Medium coordinates studio tours, offering a peek behind the creative curtains of nearly 500 local artists and makers.

#4930. New Jersey may finally reverse its draconian e-bike law after massive backlash

New Jersey lawmakers are already looking to walk back one of the country’s most controversial e-bike laws, just months after the sweeping new regulations took effect and sparked widespread backlash from riders, bicycle shops, and advocacy groups.

A newly introduced bill, S4830, sponsored by State Senator Raj Mukherji, would remove licensing, registration, and insurance requirements for low-speed electric bicycles with motors rated at 750W or less and a maximum motor-powered speed of 20 mph (32 km/h). This includes Class 1 and Class 2 electric bicycles.

The proposal would essentially restore bicycle-like treatment for these two classes of e-bikes, importantly including throttle-equipped models that make up the majority of e-bikes sold in the state and more broadly in the US, while maintaining stricter requirements for faster and more powerful electric bicycles.

The move comes after New Jersey’s previous governor, Phil Murphy, signed a sweeping e-bike law in January following several tragic crashes involving young riders.

That legislation, which took full effect in July, eliminated the state’s previous three-class e-bike framework and introduced licensing and registration requirements for all electric bicycles, including ordinary low-speed commuter models. Throttle-equipped and faster e-bikes also faced insurance requirements.

As we previously reported, New Jersey bicycle shops experienced devastating sales declines after the law took effect, with some reporting drops of 20% to 90%. Several businesses were forced to lay off employees, downsize, or even close entirely as e-bike sales and usage plummeted.

Meanwhile, riders faced confusing new requirements that made e-bikes considerably less accessible, which was particularly impactful for lower-income residents, people with disabilities, and those who rely on electric bicycles for daily transportation.

I own a Class 3 e-bike, but I also acknowledge that with its potential assisted speed of 28mph it's of a different breed than Class 1/2 bikes -- that's just too fast in crowded bike lanes or shared use paths.

The first priority should be to target reckless riding by anyone, and illegal bikes should be confiscated if they're involved.

The original law was intended to improve safety, but critics argued that it largely punished responsible riders instead of addressing the growing problem of high-powered electric motorcycles masquerading as bicycles. Only responsible riders actually registered their bikes as required by law, while others were deterred from cycling, and the hooligan riders who often gave e-bikes a bad name continued to ride illegally.

After all, requiring someone riding a 20 mph (32 km/h) commuter e-bike to obtain a driver’s license and register their bicycle does little to address reckless riding on machines capable of highway speeds.

The newly proposed legislation appears to acknowledge that distinction, offering a more targeted approach that could preserve reasonable safety regulations without making ordinary e-bike ownership unnecessarily complicated.

S4830 still needs to work its way through the legislature before becoming law, but for New Jersey’s struggling e-bike industry, it’s a promising sign that lawmakers may finally be listening. It is still widely argued that requiring electric bicycles from 21-28 mph (32-45 km/h) to require a driver’s license and registration is overly burdensome, but at least the state appears to be moving back toward the right direction.

#4929. The 87 Best Movies of 2026 So Far (and 17 More We Can't Wait For)

With Christopher Nolan, star-studded epic romance, and major Marvel events, this list is a behemoth. And it also features a movie called Behomoth!.

AS A HUGE fan and follower of both movies and sports, the former has one major advantage: There's always something to look forward to. As a fan of the New York Jets, I don't always have that luxury. As a fan of watching films, though, not only is there always something worth my time on the big screen or streaming, but there's always several flicks down the line that will sound great or intriguing for one reason or another. Will they all turn out great? Of course not. But with sports and movies alike, excitement tends to be all about hope.

After a particularly strong 2025—movies like One Battle After Another and Sinners proved to be true standouts—how does 2026 pick up? With strength and energy, that's how. The year has been filled with all sorts of great movies, from horror (like 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple and Obsession) to epics (like The Odyssey), to some pretty great comedies (like Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie and The Invite). It’s been a good year.

But we always like to look at not only what’s been good to date, but also what we can still be excited for—and that includes everything. We’re talking stuff on the calendar already, stuff we suspect could come out this year, and even stuff making noise in places like the Sundance Film Festival. Throughout the year, this list evolves from our most anticpated movies into our favorite movies, and always looks quite different in December than it did in January.

For now, we've got a lot we’ve loved, but still a lot to look forward to as well. Below, we've listed nearly 100 movies on our radar that we think you'll be really interested in seeing.

If you're interested in a killer chimpanzee B-movie slasher, then Primate is for you. The movie delivers what it promises. Ben the chimpanzee does some very terrible things on screen but it makes for quite an enjoyable experience.

28 Years Later: The Bone Temple is a direct sequel to last year's 28 Years Later. And while the great Danny Boyle doesn't return to direct, the movie is helmed by the more-than-capable Nia DaCosta and once again counts Alex Garland as its screenwriter. The real assets, though, are the movie's leads: Ralph Fiennes and Jack O'Connell are sensational, magnetic every minute they're on screen amidst all sorts of post-apocalyptic chaos. Bring on part three.

Here's a quick pitch for The Rip: Matt Damon and Ben Affleck. The longtime friends team up once again for The Rip, a cop thriller in the vein of movies you've probably seen several times before... but done particularly well. This twisty crime story involves a police team finding a ton of money in a stash house (the titular rip), and figuring out who may or may not be corrupt. In addition to our beloved duo, the cast is shockingly stacked: Steven Yeun, Kyle Chandler, Scott Adkins, and One Battle After AnotherAcademy Award nominee Teyana Taylor are all on board.

SAM RAIMI FANS, ASSEMBLE! For the first time since 2009, the master of gross effects and frenetic cameras is back with an original horror story in Send Help. Rachel McAdams plays a Survivor superfan who gets stuck on an island with her dickhead boss (Dylan O'Brien), and then the antics begin. Think Cast Away meets Misery meets Drag Me to Hell. It's great stuff.

Jason Momoa and Dave Bautista are a pair of cool dudes who, with The Wrecking Crew, set out and did exactly what they intended to do: Make a badass buddy action comedy. The movie finds the pair playing estranged brothers who reunite in Hawaii to investigate their father's mysterious death; From there, we get lots of jokes, and a lot of (very violent!!!) action. It's a good time! Honestly, if they want to make a couple sequels to this, I'd be her for it.

Part This Is Spinal Tap, part Josie and the Pussycats, and part Curb Your Enthusiasm, The Moment is Charli XCX's movie for anyone who enjoys her music, persona, or both. A really fun movie about music, stardom, and celebrity in 2026.

Pillion was technically released initially in 2025 (and was also included on our list of favorite movies of that year), but we didn't want to miss an opportunity to recommend it to people once again. This unique film follows a man (Harry Melling) who engages in an unexpected BDSM relationship with a biker he meets at random (Alexander Skarsgård). The movie is quite the tonal balancing act, at times funny, intense, raunchy, and heartbreaking, but it all comes together by the end. A delightful movie, and a great showcase for both leads.

The classic novel comes to life on the big screen once again, this time from Promising Young Woman and Saltburn director Emerald Fennell, and with Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi in the lead roles. Fennell, an Academy Award winner for her work on Promising Young Woman, has both big fans and big detractors at this point—but her take her, while book purists haven't been thrilled, is a big, visually stunning epic romance. Robbie and Elordi are both up to the task as well, bringing a charged energy to roles that really need it. Alison Oliver, who recently shined on HBO's Task, is another major highlight in a supporting role. An original soundtrack from Charli XCX helps to set the anachronistic mood and feels like a real cherry on top.

This action-thriller, based on a book by Don Winslow, looks like a very solid entry in the genre that we're going to call "Somewhat Resembles a Michael Mann Film." And reviews have confirmed as much—the movie has often been compared to Heat, and while its tough to compare to Mann's 1995 masterpiece, we'll take just about anything in that vein. Look: If someone tells you there's gonna be a crime thriller in the vein of Heat with Chris Hemsworth, Mark Ruffalo, Halle Berry, and Barry Keoghan leading the cast, how could you not be on board?

One of our coolest directors, Gore Verbinski, makes his grand return with Good Luck, Have Fun, Don't Die—his first movie since 2016's A Cure For Wellness. If you don't know Verbinski by name, you should know several of his movies: the original Pirates of the Caribbean trilogy, The Ring, and Rango are among the standouts. Good Luck follows Sam Rockwell as a man who claims to be from the future and fighting off a rogue artificial intelligence. While the movie takes some pretty tough and labored tangents into on-the-nose satire, Rockwell's performance and the practical effects keep the movie afloat as a fun sci-fi romp.

Despite the title, you don't need to know anything else about Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie before going to see it. Sure, this story of two inept dudes in a band who just want to play music at a bar—and go through all sorts of ordeals, including time-travel, to do it—has existed before in both web series and television series form. But you can just go in and laugh and the absolute madness of the movie. Trust us—you'll have a good time with this one if you're into "madness that makes you laugh." This is a long-term passion project of Matt Johnson, who became well known recently for directing the underrated BlackBerry. His brash talent is again on display here.

We're always in on Glen Powell, and How to Make a Killing marks his first foray into A24's world as a leading man. This black comedy neo-noir comes from Emily the Criminal director John Patton Ford and follows Powell as a man who decides to take out a few members of his family in order to increase his chance at making a whole bunch of money via inheritance. The supporting cast joining Powell isn't too shabby: Margaret Qualley, Jessica Henwick, Ed Harris, and Topher Grace are all along for the fun.

The Napa Boys is a very specific cut for comedy fans. Remember when all those nondescript American Pie direct-to-video sequels were coming out? "American Pie Presents:.."? The Napa Boys imagines itself as one of those: self-impressed raunchy comedy with little tact—crossed, naturally with the wine bro nature of 2004's Sideways. As a result, we get a movie that presents as a sequel to a franchise that both has long been running out of juice, and, in our world, never was. If you get the truly absurd brand of parody being presented here, you're going to get a real kick out of it.

Hoppers feels like a throwback to the golden age of Pixar. Not only is it an original story (at a time when the legendary animation studio has often been leaning into sequels, prequels, and more), but it's a sweet and funny one at that. Following an animal-lover who uses Avatar-style technology to transfer her consciousness into a robotic beaver body that can discover what animals are thinking and saying, Hoppers actually turns into something of a conspiracy mystery story. With a voice cast that includes Jon Hamm, Dave Franco, and Meryl Streep among others, it's a fun one for the whole family.

Look. This is one of those movies that you really don't have to overthink. It's Alan Ritchson—Reacher himself—taking on a giant alien robot monster thing. This is as much of a throwback to '80s-style action filmmaking as we're ever going to get, and it delivers as promised. The fact that the rest of the cast includes staples like Jai Courtney, Esai Morales, and Dennis Quaid is just a bonus.

Undertone has been positioning itself as A24's next huge horror hit—and we'll see how it lands. The filmfollows a supernatural podcaster who gets sent some particularly spooky recordings; Its soundscape has brought tons of hype, with many calling it the scariest experience of the year. A simple premise like that sometimes makes for a very solid horror movie.

Based on the novel by Andy Weir(who also wrote The Martian), Project Hail Mary is a story of space exploration and a regular guy (played by Ryan Gosling) who gets involved. Phil Lord and Chris Miller (who are responsible for the Spider-Verse movies and directed 21 Jump Street) are behind the camera for this one, which has already proven to be a big, big hit. Gosling is basically on his own out in space for most of this, but it also includes a fun little alien guy named Rocky and a nice supporting performance by Anatomy of a Fall star Sandra Hüller. The visuals here are worth seeing on the biggest screen you can find.

2018's Ready or Not was one of the most fun horror films of the last decade, and now eight years later we get to see Samara Weaving's lead sucked back into the same twisted, bloody, violent games. The supporting cast—including David Cronenberg (!!!), March MH Flex star Elijah Wood, Sarah Michelle Gellar, and The Pitt's Shawn Hatosy—is fantastic. The movie kind of keeps with the form of the first Ready or Not, so if you liked that one, you'll certainly have a fun time with this one.

Forbidden Fruits follows a secret coven of witches operating in a shopping mall. Like, guys, some premises you just have to accept for how good they are and see them out. The movie is a campy, fun, twisty turny ride. Understand what you're getting into—a little bit of horror, a little bit of satire, and some fun twists—and it's going to be a good time.

Zazie Beetz (Atlanta, Deadpool 2) gets to lead her own horror/thriller movie with They Will Kill You, where she plays a woman who answers a help-wanted ad for a housekeeper in a luxury apartment building, only to find herself fighting for her life. The movie is pretty similar in tone to fellow horror/comedy Ready or Not 2, which we just talked about. But They Will Kill You leans more into its John Wick-style action and Kill Bill style plot, which sets it apart. The supporting cast, which includes Patricia Arquette, Heather Graham, and Industry star Myha'la, make it extra worth it.

#4927. Tesla pushes Optimus deliveries to late 2027, says hands still a problem

Elon Musk now says Tesla will start delivering Optimus humanoid robots to outside customers around the end of 2027. On Tesla’s Q3 2026 earnings call, he said the hands, the robot’s durability and its AI all still need work before then.

Those are the same three problems every humanoid robot company is fighting. And despite Musk’s claim that nobody is close to Tesla, XPeng and Hyundai’s Boston Dynamics look at least as far along.

Musk said Tesla has started early production of Optimus on its high-volume line in Fremont, where Model S and Model X used to be built. Most of a line designed for a million robots a year is installed, but he said it will take at least a year, possibly longer, to reach that rate of about 20,000 units a week, because parts of the design are still changing.

He listed what has to be solved first: the AI, the electromechanical design, and reliability, including making sure the robot doesn’t wear out after a month of use. He expects those issues to be solved around the end of next year. That’s when Tesla expects to deliver Optimus to companies outside Tesla, starting with industrial jobs that are dangerous, repetitive or need to run around the clock.

When an analyst asked where the design was still changing, a Tesla Optimus engineer pointed to the hand as one of the hardest hardware areas, along with custom gears that still carry reliability concerns. Musk agreed the hand is the main issue, explaining that fingers need to be soft enough to handle delicate objects while surviving constant wear, and robots can’t heal like human skin can.

On the AI side, Musk said Tesla is still working through problems with the AI architecture. Tesla doesn’t have a fleet of robots collecting data the way its cars do for FSD, so it plans to build 10,000 to 20,000 robots and have them practice tasks in what it calls an Optimus Academy. Tesla also plans to put its AI5 chip in Optimus around April 2027.

Musk also promised an Optimus video within a month or two showing the robot doing tasks that aren’t pre-programmed. He argued that many humanoid demos look impressive but only run scripted routines that wouldn’t hold up in the real world.

None of this is new. Last month, we reported that Tesla had ramped Optimus to hundreds of units a week, but that the robots couldn’t generalize to new tasks. According to The Information, the hand and forearm had over 100 small parts assembled by hand, touch sensors had reliability issues, and the AI could take days to learn basic tasks.

Tesla’s own Q3 shareholder letter now calls the bots coming off the Fremont line “the first production-quality bots that we have manufactured.” So every Optimus before this quarter wasn’t production quality.

And the timeline keeps moving. In 2024, Musk said Tesla would build Optimus for internal use in 2025 and hopefully in high volume for other companies in 2026. In January 2025, he said Tesla would build about 10,000 robots that year, with “several thousand” doing useful work by year-end. He later admitted none were. Now external deliveries are a late-2027 target.

Musk said on the call that, to the best of his knowledge, “there is no one that is even close to Tesla” on a general-purpose humanoid robot. That’s hard to square with what competitors have shown.

XPeng turned on an automated IRON production line in Guangzhou in September, with more than 80% of core processes automated. IRON has 21 degrees of freedom in each hand and runs XPeng’s physical AI model on three in-house Turing chips. XPeng plans mass production by the end of 2026, deliveries in the first half of 2027 starting with stores and malls, and a European rollout in the second half of 2027. That’s a similar timeline to Tesla’s, if not slightly ahead.

XPeng is dealing with the same reality. It hasn’t shipped IRON to a paying customer, and its timelines have slipped like everyone else’s.

Boston Dynamics started producing its electric Atlas in January and says all of its 2026 Atlas deployments are already committed, including to Google DeepMind, whose AI models Boston Dynamics is integrating. In September, it opened a robot training center at Hyundai’s Metaplant in Georgia, where Atlas is learning to sequence car parts for the assembly line. Hyundai plans to deploy 25,000 robots across its plants over the next few years. Boston Dynamics also hired Milan Kovac, Tesla’s former head of Optimus, in January.

Boston Dynamics also seems to have taken a more pragmatic approach to hands, prioritizing sturdy grippers for industrial parts over human-like dexterity. That’s less impressive in a demo, but it’s a way around the problem Tesla says is holding it back.

My prediction is that Optimus will forever be delayed. It's like FSD in Tesla cars -- never quite good enough to really take off and always one or two releases away from fixing everything. At least a Tesla car with a broken FSD is still a good car. Optimus with a bad brain is an interesting-looking boat anchor.

Tesla is facing the exact same problems as everyone else: hands that survive real work, robots reliable enough to pay for themselves, and AI that can handle tasks it wasn’t trained for. Nobody has cracked generalization yet, Tesla included. The difference is that Tesla’s competitors aren’t telling investors they have no competition.

Based on what’s public, XPeng has a more automated production line and a similar delivery date, and Boston Dynamics is already deploying Atlas in a car factory with committed customers. Tesla may well have the most robots built, but building robots isn’t the hard part. Making them useful is.

Tesla has promised a video in a month or two. If it shows Optimus doing real, unscripted work, great. But we’ve heard about Optimus timelines before, and they’ve slipped every year. Why should late 2027 be different?

#4926. 9 Decorative Plates for When You’re Sick of Boring Dishes (2026)

The great thing about decorative plates is that they don’t have to disappear into storage once dinner is over. Hang a few on a display wall, prop one up alongside your favorite cookbooks, or work it into a shelf vignette. So what makes one worth showing off? It’s the details that spark curiosity: A scalloped edge, an unexpected color combination, or a hand-painted pattern can be enough to make you look twice. “Any plate with a fun decorative motif, bright colors, or an interesting shape makes it more than just simply functional,” says Olympia Irving of Carolina Irving and Daughters.

Of course, good looks are only part of the appeal. The most memorable pieces often have a story to tell, whether it's a vintage find or a handmade design with charming, artisanal characteristics. Even the finish can make a statement. “I often find myself drawn to surfaces that look like they’re in flux—glazes that pool unpredictably or finishes that suggest movement or fragility, even in solid materials," says Anna Jewsbury, founder and artistic director of Completedworks.

There's no need to play by the rules of a perfectly coordinated table setting. A little contrast, a playful border, or a beautifully imperfect finish makes for a more interesting table—one that looks like it can only belong to you. Ahead, nine decorative plates that deserve a spot in your regular rotation and perhaps a prime position in your china cabinet.

For a plate that has lived a completely different life before landing on your table—try this Completedworks dinner plate made from recycled glass. Sculptural and subversive, it’s made from broken windows, melted glass bottles, and is repurposed using old engine oil as fuel. “We often play with the illusion of softness in materials that are anything but. Those moments where form and material are at odds are the ones I find most compelling,” Jewsbury shares.

Transport yourself to the French countryside with this Villeroy & Boch dinner plate. Part of the brand's beloved French Garden collection, its fruit-and-floral motif brings to mind sun-drenched afternoons and leisurely lunches in the garden. Even fruit and pastries straight from the market become a proper breakfast spread.

Does anything scream summer more than seashell-shaped splatterware? These Concha plates by editor-favorite brand Carolina Irving & Daughters bring together a seaside-inspired shape along with a creative, painterly glaze. “It is easy to make a table setting pop by only adding a few decorative touches, whether that is a dessert plate or a salt dish,” Irving says. “Truthfully, we love to use decorative plates as our everyday dishware!”

Designed by Cabana and made by heritage Italian porcelain house Ginori 1735, the painterly motif on this plate adds an element of rustic Italian charm to the table. Whether you stick to a cohesive set or play around with different colors and patterns for a more collected vibe, these plates can make even the simplest meals feel storied.

Score a vintage-inspired find without the hours spent scouring antique shops. Olive Ateliers did the legwork with its Vincent Plate, featuring a hand-painted floral motif, an aged blue rim, and an ivory ceramic base. “A plate becomes more when it holds history,” shares Laura Sotelo, cofounder of Olive Ateliers. “The wow isn’t about perfection—it’s about the story it carries. It should feel like it belonged somewhere long before it ended up in your hands.”

Each plate is finished by hand, giving it the character of a one-of-a-kind discovery. One important distinction: This plate isn't food-safe, so save it for a spot on your wall or shelf instead.

The soft pale yellow tones and the scalloped edge are some of our favorite details about these affordable, vintage-inspired dinner plates by West Elm. They are perfect for those who want to dip their toes into a decorative tabletop but aren’t quite ready to splurge. Sold in sets of four or eight, they strike the right balance between charm and accessibility and are ideal for daily use.

Your next dinner party can channel a little Italian holiday spirit with these hand-painted plates, crafted in a family-run workshop on the Amalfi Coast. They're available in two sizes, so they're equally charming piled high with antipasti or passed around the table with the main course.

A collaboration between L’Objet and South African artist Ruan Hoffmann, this plate brings a bold edge to the table with its painterly design and hand-applied 24k gold accents. While it’s smaller in dimension (6.5 inches), it makes a subtle statement and can complement your existing dinnerware.

If you're a fan of British artist Tess Newall's painterly work, you'll be happy to know her designs have made their way to the dinner table, courtesy of Porta. The “Ottoline” plates take inspiration from English country houses, Bloomsbury interiors, and traditional European ceramics. These hand-painted pieces are adorned with scrolling motifs, dotted edges, and wave detailing in contrasting shades of slate blue and rust. Sold in sets of four, they're a lovely way to bring a little art history to the table.

#4923. Scania speeds up switch to silent semi trucks with Chinese production

Record fuel prices driven by the dumb oil war in Iran are fueling a faster-than-expected switch to electric trucks in the world’s biggest freight markets – and Swedish heavy truck brand Scania is scrambling to meet demand with a new factory in China.

The Swedish truck maker is set to begin manufacturing battery-powered trucks at its new Rugao assembly plant in China as soon as it receives a license, according to Scania Chief Executive Officer Christian Levin.

In an interview given in Södertälje, Levin said he expects electric trucks to account for half of China’s market by the start of 2027. “We have had to speed up those plans,” Levin explained. “It’s always quite a complicated process in China to get licenses to do different things, but I have to say: it has moved much faster than we could have imagined.”

Chinese electric semi truck sales jumped almost 80% in the first half of 2026 compared to 2025, and are on pace to account for over 30% of all heavy trucks sold this year – and that pace is accelerating.

Scania built the Rugao plant last year, making it the Traton Group’s first wholly-owned factory in China. The facility will serve China and other Asian markets, and can build both conventional diesel and battery-powered trucks on the same assembly lines.

“It is happening quickly and that’s incredibly fascinating,” says Levin. “It means they have found a way to expand charging infrastructure and the power grid, which is the biggest challenge we have in many countries around the world, including Europe.”

I would respond to that by saying that China has a political system that makes the betterment of society as-a-whole a higher priority than corporate profits and creating shareholder value, but nobody asked me and this isn’t one of those blogs. Instead, I’ll open the floor to the smartest commenters in the biz: Electrek readers, let us know what you think!

#4921. Where Does Mark Ruffalo Live? Exploring the Avengers Star’s East Coast Real Estate Portfolio

He’s currently booked and busy, but where does Mark Ruffalo live when he’s not filming? The Oscar-nominated actor appears in five projects released in 2026 alone. With that kind of schedule, the Marvel star must maintain somewhat of a nomadic lifestyle. It’s no surprise that, for years, he and his family juggled properties on both coasts. These days, however, Ruffalo is all in on New York—when he isn’t traveling to movie sets around the world.

Read on to learn more about the Emmy winner’s homes in the Big Apple and beyond.

Ruffalo first visited the small town of Callicoon in New York’s Catskills region in the mid-’90s, according to a December 2017 interview with Men’s Journal. Soon after visiting Callicoon, the 28-year-old Ruffalo paid $63,000 for his first home there: a 27-acre property with a one-room cabin. Around 2001, he and his wife, Sunrise Coigney, purchased an estate there, on a former dairy farm, complete with a 19th-century post-and-beam barn. “Smartest thing I ever did,” the Avengers star told the outlet. “It ended up having a huge influence on my life in many ways.”

At first, Ruffalo and his family split their time between their Los Angeles and New York homes. But that soon began to wear on the couple, spurring the decision to sell their Hollywood Hills property in 2009 and move to Callicoon full-time. “The whole guiding idea for living upstate was to simplify,” Ruffalo said in 2011. “It’s the best thing I could have done for the family as a whole and for the kids in particular.… We’re letting them grow a little wild, and we step in when it’s essential, but in a lot of ways they figure stuff out on their own and they do it in a way that’s organic and lasts. We live in a small space and we’re really influenced by the natural world around us. Eventually that is reflected in one’s behavior and how you get along with other people. There’s a lot of quiet in the country and there’s a lot of quietness in my children.”

On the farm, the Now You See Me actor built a woodworking shop, a chicken coop, and a garden where he grows “strawberries, rhubarb, tomatoes, basil, corn, a beautiful asparagus bed that’s five years old, and watermelon, which is hard to grow up here,” Ruffalo said. “Now I’m doing a little orchard—raspberries, Honeycrisp apples, blueberries, a pear tree.”

In the mid-2010s, Ruffalo moved his primary residence back to NYC for his children’s schooling. “We try to get to our place in Callicoon as much as we can on weekends and holidays and of course spend the summer there,” he said in 2016. “It’s a place I will always hold dear to my heart, filled with lifetime friends and lots of positive sentiment. It’s the best, most-interlocked community I have ever lived in save for my early years in Los Angeles when I was scraping it out to become an actor.” Ruffalo also compared the region to his upbringing in Wisconsin: “Just outside my town there were vast dairy farms, where I would spend my days as a boy fishing in creeks and ponds or climbing around in trees. Everything about the Catskills reminds me of my childhood. I don’t know what to say other than it’s my home.”

Ruffalo and Coigney were married in 2000 and welcomed the first of three children the following year. In December 2004, a few months before their second child was born, the growing family moved into a three-bedroom Hollywood Hills home that they bought for about $1.7 million. It was the cherry on top of a banner year for Ruffalo, who appeared in four movies that year: We Don’t Live Here Anymore, Eternal Sunshine of the Spotless Mind, 13 Going on 30, and Collateral.

According to The Los Angeles Times, the gated single-story residence dated to 1933 and was built in the style of a Connecticut farmhouse. Across 3,000 square feet, it hosted three bedrooms, four bathrooms, and a detached guesthouse. Highlights included a fireplace-warmed living room with beamed ceilings, and a breakfast room.

Ruffalo and Coigney welcomed their third child in 2007, then sold the Los Angeles house for $1.65 million two years later to New Girl star Zooey Deschanel and her then husband, Death Cab for Cutie frontman Ben Gibbard. Deschanel and Gibbard divorced in 2012, and Deschanel sold the property in 2015, the Los Angeles Times reported.

Ruffalo’s next real estate purchase was a $3.5 million town house in Brooklyn’s Carroll Gardens neighborhood in 2015. Spanning 3,000 square feet, the 1899-built residence had four bedrooms, three bathrooms, a backyard garden, and dreamy period details: cathedral ceilings, marble fireplaces, decorative arches, and wide-plank wood flooring. There was also a large kitchen with a high-end range, a sunroom with a balcony, and a curved wooden staircase leading to the upstairs bedrooms.

The actor sold the Brooklyn home in 2017 for $3.2 million—a slight loss.

Mark Ruffalo at a rally for the Landmark West Park Presbyterian Church in 2025

Though he left Brooklyn, Ruffalo was not leaving NYC altogether—just moving across the river to Manhattan. In January 2017, he and Coigney were seen touring a $9.99 million six-bedroom town house on the Upper West Side. While it doesn’t appear that they signed that deed, the couple did pick up a home in the neighborhood at some point thereafter. The Poor Things star has been a vocal supporter of upholding the landmark status of the neighborhood’s historic West Park Presbyterian Church since 2023. In December 2025, he said that he lived “a block away from the church.” While the details of this real estate transaction are unknown, Ruffalo’s Upper West Side residence is reportedly an early-20th-century four-story town house.

#4920. Matthew McConaughey and Woody Harrelson Embrace a Very Real Family Rumor in Apple TV’s Brothers

The new series reunites the True Detective duo for some meta sitcom chaos.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

EVERYONE KNOWS THAT Matthew McConaughey and Woody Harrelson are one of the great duos in show business. After meeting and becoming friends on the set of the 1999 comedy film EdTV, the actors began a long friendship, along the way collaborating again for the first season of True Detective, widely considered to be one of the great television shows of all time.

But did you know that in addition to being great friends, the two might also be half-relatives? That’s the premise of Apple TV’s new comedy series Brothers, which reunites the duo on screen for the first time since 2014’s True Detective, and pulls from a bit of very real mythology that the two only relatively recently discovered on their own. “Matthew’s mother, I’d known her 25 years, and she told me, ‘I knew your father.’ You know, like, with the ellipses: ‘I knew… your father’” Harrelson told Variety. “She tells me, and I’m thinking, ‘What’s behind this story?’ And then I find out they weren’t just friends—there was a little more to it.”

Brothers, in turn, stars McConaughey and Harrelson as fictional versions of themselves exploring this possibility. Think of it as the meta-nature of Curb Your Enthusiasm, combined with the goofiness of a family sitcom like Schitt’s Creek or Malcolm in the Middle, and, of course, the star power of True Detective.

Now, just because they made the show doesn’t mean that McConaughey believes the story. “Woody thinks he knows that we’re brothers, and it’s not that I don’t want to know—I don’t believe it,” McConaughey told Variety at the show’s premiere. “My mom’s the one who started this whole damn thing, by saying ‘I knew… your father… with the long pause after that. Even my five-year-old son understood the innuendo of what that meant.”

So, are the A-listers actually brothers? You figure they could just take a DNA test and figure out once and for all. But for now, that doesn’t seem to be in the cards. “I’ve been wanting to, but he refuses,” Harrelson told The Hollywood Reporter. McConaughey then added that maybe that could change in the future—but he’s a little frightened about what it could mean. “I’ll probably come around to it,” he said. “If it’s true, I’ve got a lot to unpack. If it’s not true, he’s not going to be near as fun to hang out with.”

There’s a lot to figure out for these two. But in the meantime, why not see how fictional Matthew and Woody handle it? Below, we’ve got all the information to make sure you don’t miss an episode of Apple TV’s Brothers.

The final episode of Brothers season 1 will officially come out on Wednesday, November 4. But as with all Apple TV shows, it'll release a bit early the night prior. So if you're eager, keep an eye out for the next episode on the evening of Tuesday, November 3.

The debut season of Brothers will run for eight episodes, with episodes rolling out on a weekly basis, the first seven of which have now aired. That means there’s still one more week—and one episodes of Matthew and Woody’s brotherly (or not-so-brotherly) chaos—remaining in the show’s first season.
