#4918. 7 Best Sex Blankets for Mess-Free Fun, According to Sex Experts

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Let's face it: Any kind of sex is likely going to involve fluids. And while that's totally normal, nothing kills the mood quite like dealing with that dreaded wet spot on your bed sheets immediately after having sex. There is a way to avoid that annoyance completely, though—I'm talking about sex blankets.

Sex blankets let you get as messy as you want during sex without worrying about the cleanup afterward. Aside from the fact that they help protect your sheets from lube, ejaculate, period blood, and more, they can also make sex even hotter by creating a more relaxed environment. "The more comfortable our environment is, the less things we’re worried about [and] the easier it is to relax and release into pleasure," says Jenn Gunsaullus, PhD, a sociologist, intimacy and relationship coach, and author of From Madness to Mindfulness: Reinventing Sex for Women. "A sex blanket can help make us more comfortable, and reduce any stress about being judged, making a mess, or messing up something nice."

While sex blankets come in a variety of styles and sizes, the best ones have a few characteristics in common. Generally, you'll want to look for a blanket that's waterproof and designed to protect your mattress topper/couch/floor/countertop from any fluids. It's also best to look for a product that's machine-washable and a great size for your needs. Ahead, sex therapists and sex educators unpack the top traits to look for in a sex blanket.

What really sets a sex blanket aside from a regular one is its capacity to reduce mess. So, you'll need a waterproof product, and it'll help if the blanket is also absorbent, says Goody Howard, a Dallas-based sex educator. "The best sex blanket is going to be one that has good absorption, not just something that is waterproof and doesn't allow things to get wet." She recommends looking for keywords like 'charcoal' and 'microfiber' in the product description. This will let you know it's actually absorbent instead of just a plastic-lined piece of fabric, Howard says.

Size is also an important feature to consider. Some sex blankets don’t cover the entirety of a bed, so if you’re looking to cover a lot of surface area, you'll want to opt for a larger blanket like the Liberator Fascinator Throw. On the other hand, a smaller sex blanket, like the Playdrop Sex Mat, might be more travel-friendly and easier to toss in the wash. It all depends on your needs.

If you plan on keeping your sex blanket out in plain sight, you probably don't want it to stick out like a sore thumb. "Keep in mind where the blanket will live in your space so that the color or design matches the area," Howard says. A product that matches your sheets, comforter, or pillows might also make the whole experience of using a sex blanket feel sexier and more natural.

For the past year, Women’s Health editors vetted the bestselling, top-rated sex blankets on Amazon and beyond. After consulting two sex experts, we personally tested each blanket by pouring a cup of water on it and watching how the material responded. We also sent blankets home with our team of anonymous reviewers for further testing, and evaluated thousands of customer reviews and ratings to narrow down the best sex blankets for a wide range of needs and preferences.

So, if you’re interested in trying it out (or looking for an upgrade), see below for the seven best sex blankets you can buy right now, curated with help from sex experts and tested by real users.

Though this is the priciest option on this list, our testers and experts agree that this sex blanket is also the most luxurious and versatile. Not only does it feature soft and plush microvelvet on both sides, but the Liberator Fascinator also comes in multiple colors and sizes—all of which are fully waterproof. Plus, it has a protective inner liner that locks in moisture, ensuring your bed stays spotless and dry.

According to testers, it does take a while to dry out after a wash, but other than that they love this blanket. One even said that it dramatically enhanced their sex life because it's much more romantic than the towels they were using before purchasing this throw blanket.

Literally as soft as sheep's wool, this blanket is perfect for couples who like getting cozy… in more ways than one (wink). The plush micro fleece side of the blanket is waterproof, while the sherpa side is not, so just keep that in mind before laying it down on your bed. The fabric is thicker and warmer than most sex blankets, and it also comes in four sizes and nine colors, so you can customize this comfy throw to your style.

Our tester says that when she's not having sex on this blanket, she loves to get cozy underneath it on her couch or at her desk. It really is a great multipurpose blanket if you're looking for something you can use anywhere at any time. No one will know you use it during sexy time, too!

Sure, this is technically a waterproof blanket for pets, but that's actually what makes it perfect for enjoying sex without worrying about fluids ruining your bed. #4916. 7 Best Lumbar Pillows Bolster Your Back and Bedscape (2026)

Though a lumbar pillow is typically the last detail in making a bed, it’s often the first thing you or a guest will notice. Because it’s long and low, its best suited to the space in front of loftier throw pillows, making a horizon line across your bedscape, and it can bring in a color or texture the rest of the room is vying for. The shape turns up all over AD’s archives, including in supermodel Paloma Elsesser’s Brooklyn home and this classically decorated Southern house.

A lumbar pillow can mean two things: a firm or cushioned gel pillow designed to support the lower back, or an elegant decorative detail among your bedding. While we like both, we’ll leave the Cushion Lab’s best-of for another day. Here, we’re focused on a more inspirational lineup, between linen, wool, velvet, and cotton lumbar pillows that pull a bed and sofa together (and still give your lower back something to lean on in an accent chair). Below are the seven we like best.

Sometimes one pillow is all a bed really needs. West Elm’s oversized linen pillow cover has the scale to anchor a queen or king bed on its own, creating a clean, pulled-together look without a full stack of throw pillows. The linen keeps it loose, making it especially well-suited to coastal, minimalist, modern farmhouse, and casual contemporary spaces.

This pillow is all about texture. Its dimensional surface gives a sofa or bed a more layered, considered look without relying on a loud print or saturated color. In other words, its style will speak volumes even when paired with quieter neutrals.

At 48 inches, Quince’s velvet lumbar is long enough to span a queen bed with nothing else in front of it, and the cotton pile catches light in a way linen won’t. The glacier blue reads cool and a little silvery against white or oatmeal bedding. It’s dry clean only, and the insert is sold separately.

Color is the point with this Athena cushion. Rather than fading quietly into the background, this lumbar pillow is designed to bring energy to a room, whether it’s breaking up the monotony of a neutral living room or adding contrast to a more restrained bedding scheme. It’s the kind of accent that makes a pillow arrangement feel more collected, playful, and intentional.

A little color can go a long way, especially in the form of Pendleton’s Turquoise Crosses Lumbar Pillow. Its vivid palette and geometric pattern give neutral furniture or a plain accent chair an instant lift. For anyone drawn to Southwestern-inspired textiles, this pick brings just enough graphic punch without crowding the rest of the room.

The Mendocino cover is made up of hand-dyed washed linen, so the color shifts slightly across the surface instead of sitting flat. What separates it from the other linen picks here is the fringe: three-inch knotted tassels run along two sides, which give a simple lumbar some movement at the ends of a sofa or bed. Nota bene: the insert is sold separately.

Washed linen has a way of making a room feel more relaxed and breezy, which is exactly the appeal of Brooklinen’s pillow cover. The simple design is easy to fold into an existing pillow arrangement, while the softly textured fabric and choice of vibrant hues keep it from feeling too plain.

A lumbar pillow is a long, rectangular cushion designed to support the lower back. The term describes two different products: one is the ergonomic back support pillow, often memory foam, made for an office chair or car seat. The other is the decorative version on this list, which works as an accent piece on beds, sofas, benches, and accent chairs. Their shape makes them especially useful for layering with standard square throw pillows, adding dimension without making a space feel cluttered.

One of the easiest ways to style a lumbar pillow is to place it in front of larger pillows or shams on a bed. On a sofa or accent chair, a lumbar pillow can serve as a single focal point or be paired with smaller throw pillows in complementary colors, patterns, or textures. For a more polished look, choose a lumbar pillow that either picks up a color already in the room or brings in a contrasting texture, like linen, wool, velvet, or tassels.

For lower back support, a lumbar pillow goes in the curve between your back and the back of a sofa, reading chair, or recliner chair. Keep in mind a firmer, fuller back pillow will likely do more for your posture than a soft, decorative one. For styling, it can also be centered across a bed, layered in front of sleeping pillows, or placed on a bench for extra comfort and visual interest. Oversized lumbar pillows work especially well on queen and king beds because they can anchor the entire pillow arrangement with one clean horizontal line.

#4913. Volkswagen is launching its most affordable EV yet in 2027, starting at around €20,000

Volkswagen’s upcoming electric car will be smaller and lower-priced than the ID. Polo, starting at around €20,000 ($22,500).

The new Electric Urban Car Family is the Volkswagen Group’s answer to Chinese automakers like BYD: a new lineup of compact, entry-level electric cars.

The lineup currently includes the Volkswagen ID. Polo, ID. Cross, plus the Cupra Raval and Skoda Epiq, all based on the same MEB+ platform.

After launching the lower-priced base versions of the ID. Polo and ID. Cross, starting at €24,995 and €27,995, respectively, Volkswagen is preparing to introduce an even smaller, more affordable EV in 2027.

Volkswagen revealed the ID. EVERY1 concept car last March, a preview of its upcoming entry-level EV that will arrive in 2027 starting at around €20,000.

The ID. EVERY1, which is expected to go on sale as the ID. Up! next year, is “the last piece of the puzzle,” VW brand CEO Thomas Schäfer said, as it aims to offer the widest selection in the volume segment.

With a length of 3,880 mm, the entry-level EV is positioned between the former Up! (3,600 mm) and ID. Polo (4,053 mm). Although that is tiny, VW said it will offer space for four passengers and a luggage compartment volume of 305 liters.

Volkswagen has yet to reveal details, but the €20,000 EV is expected to use the same 37 kWh LFP battery as the base ID. Polo and ID. Cross to keep costs down.

The ID. Polo with the 37 kWh battery has a WLTP range of up to 334 km (207 miles), so the smaller model will likely offer around the same or slightly more range.

Volkswagen said it will offer at least 250 km (155 miles) of range. It will also use a new 70 kW (94 hp) electric motor, enabling a top speed of 130 km/h (80 mph).

The production version of the ID. EVERY1 will be one of nine new models VW plans to launch by 2027.

Volkswagen delivered on its promise to launch the ID. Polo with the 37 kWh battery for under €25,000. Will it do the same with the new entry-level model at around €20,000? We will find out shortly.

It will be produced in Portugal, this has been confirmed ages ago.

According to Spanish auto magazine Autofácil, Volkswagen could manufacture the €20,000 EV in Spain, although Volkswagen has yet to confirm a production site.

The Group currently produces the ID. Polo and Cupra Raval at its Martorell plant, while the ID. Cross and Skoda Epiq are manufactured at the Navarra plant, both in Spain.

Volkswagen’s new Electric Urban Car Family is off to a promising start, securing nearly 150,000 orders after series production began in June.

While those in the US will miss out on the new entry-level EV, Volkswagen is launching the ID. Tiguan in 2027 as a replacement for the ID.4.

#4908. Skeleton Architecture: 7 Buildings That Prove Why Bones Are Some Designer’s Favorite Muse

“It should be harsh and cruel, as if made of bones,” Antoni Gaudí wrote next to his sketch of the Passion Façade, the west-facing entrance of his magnum opus, the Sagrada Família. In stark contrast to the organic, pictorial design of the Nativity Façade on the opposite side of the basilica, which depicts the birth of Jesus, Gaudí employed skeletal forms to evoke human suffering and Christ’s final days.

While the Catalan architect drew inspiration from skeletons for multiple projects—most notably Barcelona’s Casa Batlló, a residential building known as the House of Bones for its dragon-vertebrae-inspired design, and Casa Milà, with its undulating, rib cage–like arches—he was not the first to do so. Skeletons have long served as sacred ornamentation across cultures, representing the transience of life. As early as 7,200 BCE, Middle Eastern communities displayed human skulls in domestic spaces to honor their ancestors, while in Mesoamerica, the Aztecs erected elaborate skull racks known as tzompantli to display sacrificial captives’ remains as a demonstration of power. Following the Black Death in medieval Europe, memento mori, symbolic reminders of death’s inevitability, further ubiquitized skulls in art and architecture. Meanwhile, as European cemeteries became increasingly inundated from the Middle Ages into the early modern period, carcasses were exhumed and relocated to crypts featuring skeletal decorative displays, such as Portugal’s 16th-century Capela dos Ossos and the Czech Republic’s Sedlec Ossuary.

By the late 19th and early 20th centuries, architects such as Antoni Gaudí began studying skeletal anatomy not merely for adornment, but as the actual framework for buildings. After all, much like the human body, a building relies on its skeleton as the foundation for life within. From an avant-garde temple to to contemporary buildings constructed to resemble skeletal forms, below are architectural marvels that reveal why skeletons remain an enduring muse for architects.

While historic ossuaries incorporated actual human remains into their architecture, contemporary designer and artist Chalermchai Kositpipat incorporated ornamental skeletal architecture into Wat Rong Khun, or the White Temple, an unconventional Buddhist temple and art installation that depicts the stages of Nirvana. To enter the temple, visitors must cross a bridge over a sea of sculpted hands and skulls, symbolizing human desire and the cycle of suffering on the path to enlightenment. Started in 1997 and still evolving, the complex features intricate white ornamentation and mirrored glass symbolizing purity and wisdom.

Moving beyond skeletal ornamentation, Antoni Gaudí helped pioneer Catalan Modernism, a late 19th- and early 20th-century architectural movement that challenged traditional academicism, rejecting rigid geometry in favor of nature’s organic forms and structural principles, including those of skeletal anatomy. Nicknamed the House of Bones, Gaudí’s Casa Batlló, which was created from 1904–1906, is among the most prominent examples of this movement. Its undulating façade features balconies resembling skulls, slender bone-like columns, and a roof covered in iridescent scales that evokes a dragon’s spine. Originally designed as a private residence for textile industrialist Josep Batlló, the UNESCO-listed building is now a museum open to visitors, its skeletal framework inspiring interpretations ranging from human anatomy to the legend of Saint George slaying the dragon.

Built between 1906 and 1912, another of Gaudí’s architectural masterpieces, Casa Milà, also known as La Pedrera, draws inspiration from natural forms, most notably in what’s known as the Whale-Bone Attic, where 270 repeating brick arches expand and contract in size to resemble the rib cage of a giant whale. These catenary arches support the building’s rippling roof, demonstrating Gaudí’s fascination with the relationship between anatomy and engineering. More broadly, Gaudí conceived the building as an animal body, with internal supporting columns serving as its bones and the sculptural stone façade as its skin. Originally constructed as a residential apartment building, the UNESCO World Heritage Site remains partly residential, while its attic, rooftop with surreal sculptural chimneys, and other historic spaces are open to visitors.

Designed by Spanish architect Santiago Calatrava and completed in 1994, the Lyon-Saint Exupéry Airport Railway Station combines skeletal engineering with the imagery of a bird taking flight. “Sometimes I say [I’m inspired by] the human skeleton, because it’s true that there’s something essential in the construction of our body,” Calatrava once told Fast Company in 2010. (He’s also inspired by nature more broadly, he added.)

This idea is on full display at the Lyon-Saint Exupéry Airport Railway Station, where the expansive central hall features a soaring steel framework of intersecting arches, while the adjoining railway platforms are sheltered beneath a vaulted concrete roof supported by rib-like structures. The building’s curved, tapering spine and wing-shaped silhouette evoke avian anatomy.

Another Calatrava design, the Quadracci Pavilion at the Milwaukee Art Museum, completed in 2001, emulates a mythical winged skeletal form. Its soaring Windhover Hall features repeating white concrete ribs, pointed arches, and a 90-foot-high glass ceiling, evoking a Gothic cathedral with the anatomy of a fantastical creature. Outside, the building’s most recognizable feature, the Burke Brise Soleil, consists of 72 steel fins that open and close like enormous wings, spanning 217 feet when fully extended.

The World Trade Center Oculus resembles an enormous skeletal structure through its repeating white steel ribs, which extend outward to form a pair of wing-like canopies. Another Calatrava project, the architect conceived it as a symbol of hope and renewal following the September 11 attacks—the transportation hub was inspired by the image of a bird being released from a child’s hands. Inside, its soaring, cathedral-like hall features an elliptical framework of steel and glass, with a central skylight that floods the space with natural light. The structure’s combination of skeletal anatomy and avian symbolism is one of the most renowned projects embodying Calatrava’s exploration of biological forms in architecture.

The 61,000 meter headquarters for Chinese fashion company Xinhee, designed by Beijing-based MAD Architects and opened in 2017, conjures the architecture of the human body. Conceived of as a structure of “skin and bones,” the building features a diagonal steel skeleton enveloped in translucent PTFE membrane resembling fabric draped over the human body, while also filtering natural light and facilitating natural ventilation in the subtropical climate. Its flower-like layout consists of six radiating wings, each housing a different atrium, which are connected by a suspended footbridge that doubles as a fashion runway. With its elevated gardens and multistory green spaces, the structure integrates nature into the building’s skeletal framework. Known as one of the most innovative buildings in contemporary China, the design personifies the relationship between fashion and architecture.

#4906. Kia believes it has a way to beat China’s cheap EVs in Canada

Kia is betting its new lineup of affordable EVs, like the EV3, can beat the incoming wave of Chinese models in Canada, but affordability is only one part of the equation.

After launching the EV3 in Canada in August, Kia said it was the lowest-priced electric vehicle on the market, starting at CAD $39,744.

It may not hold the title for long, though. Canada struck a deal with China earlier this year, allowing up to 49,000 electric vehicles to enter the Canadian market at a 6.1% tariff rate.

According to Kia Canada’s COO, Elias El-Achhab, the company sees the deal’s timing as an opportunity.

“I thought it was just a bonus,” El-Achhab said during Automotive News Canada’s Retail Forum this week.

Kia is “trying to be the first affordable EV brand in the country,” he said, but that doesn’t necessarily mean just offering the lowest-priced vehicle. The Korean automaker is focusing on offering EVs at an affordable price that still come with the tech and features buyers want.

The EV3 (check out our review) joins Kia’s other mass-market electric vehicles, including the EV4 and EV5, that are now on sale in Canada.

When asked whether Kia could face more competition from China because of its positioning as a low-cost brand, El-Achhab said: “I actually think the opposite.”

“Because we are more affordable … and I will reiterate, it’s more of a value equation that we offer,” he explained during the Forum.

While Kia still offers vehicles across all powertrains, El-Achhab said that around 90% of those who buy an EV tend to stick with it.

At Kia’s national dealer meeting last week, he said the company’s vision had plenty of support, adding that orders for the EV3 and EV5 are already backed up.

The EV5, Kia’s midsize electric SUV, starts at CAD $43,495, and the EV4 starts at CAD $38,995 in Canada.

Although Kia delayed the EV4 for the US “indefinitely” and has no plans to bring the EV5 to the States, the EV3 is now on sale. Starting at $29,890, the 2027 EV3 is among the most affordable electric vehicles currently available in the US.

The 2027 Kia EV3 is available in four trims with front-wheel-drive and all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain options in the US and Canada.

Buyers can choose from two battery pack options. The lowest-priced EV3 Light FWD model uses a 58.3 kWh battery and delivers up to 221 miles of range. The Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT use a larger 81.4 kWh battery that delivers up to 321 miles of range on a single charge.

Inside, the interior features Kia’s new ccNC (connected car Navigation Cockpit) with standard wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

The setup includes nearly 30″ of screen space combined, including dual 12.3″ driver display and infotainment screens, and an added 5″ climate control display.

They will be fine until various Chery sub brands, geely, byd show up with extreme values. Ev3 seems to be doing ok in Europe as maybe there is some brand loyalty. I haven't heard good things about Kia dealers in Canada. If geely can bring the ex2 in for under 30k cad. I'm not sure ev3 can hold it's own. Kia might have to reduce prices then like they have done and then sure. They just need to bring value.

All EV3 variants come with a built-in NACS port so that drivers can charge at Tesla Superchargers without a separate adapter.

The 58.3 kWh battery can charge from 10% to 80% in 29 minutes using a 350 kW DC fast charger, while the 81.4 kWh battery takes around 31 minutes.

All EV3 models come with standard i-Pedal 3.0 one-pedal driving, vehicle-to-load (V2L), and Smart Cruise Control (SCC).

#4905. They’ve Taken on Clayface, the Guardians of the Galaxy, and Star Trek. But Can They Survive Each Other?

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Collin Kelly and Jackson Lanzing are some of the busiest comic book writers in the business—and their deep, complex, and long-standing friendship and working relationship is the biggest reason why.

RELATIONSHIPS ARE HARD. Interpersonal dynamics of any sort require work to maintain—Ben Affleck took a lot of shit for saying his marriage was work, but he was right. A myriad of different factors can destabilize a dynamic between people at any given moment: ego, money, miscommunication, and on the list goes.

Unless you’re Collin Kelly and Jackson Lanzing.

The comic writing duo, collectively and colloquially known as “Hivemind,” have worked on major properties including, but certainly not limited to, Captain America, Guardians of the Galaxy, Star Trek, and Thunderbolts. That’s not to mention their creator-owned work, like the new horror comic, You’ll Never Leave This Place Alive, which is akin to Marvel’s Runaways meets M. Night Shyamalan’s The Village. And right now, their work on the 2022 one-shot Batman: One Bad Day: Clayface, is part of the DNA that makes up Clayface—the latest production from James Gunn’s DC Studios and the current #1 movie in America. These are the kinds of projects that most writers would kill to be a part of. And, at every step, this pair has done it together.

In a profession where it often feels like a Battle Royale-style competition to land a paid gig, how and why do these two decide to work together?

The answer dates back to 2005, when the pair met at the University of Southern California’s famed film school. Lanzing remembers being on his very first film set and passing by a sound guy, David Server, talking about the Avengers. After doubling back and clarifying if he was, indeed, talking about the comic, the pair hit it off. Not long after, Lanzing went over to Server’s place to hang out, where he met Kelly, who was Server’s roommate. “We met briefly and became friendly,” Lanzing says. “I was definitely—let’s say—a little more hubristic back then,” Kelly states. “I think I was a little bit high on my own supply and wasn’t really quite as engaging with other folks as much.” Kelly went off to Ireland to study abroad and “came back and discovered that this guy, [Lanzing], was still in our lives. I really thought it was going to be a short, summer fling.”

The trio would pile in a PT Cruiser lovingly nicknamed “The Beast,” because it would “growl anytime you tried to gas it” and trek down to local comic book shops Golden Apple or Meltdown to pick up new issues for the week. The trio would then go to a greasy pizza place, read their books, and talk about them. “Because we were all film students, we’re breaking apart the story,” Kelly says. “We were really doing our best to effectively just be passionate nerds within the space. Once you spend enough time with someone and see their real passion for something, it's hard not to start to bond. Once you start to see that passion in someone's eyes and understand what they care about, that's how real relationships are built.”

In a way, Kelly’s relationship with Server developed as a Step Brothers-esque “Did we just become best friends” moment. But his relationship with Lanzing had more of the slow-burn nature of a courting.

“It genuinely was,” Kelly confirms. “Absolutely.”

If hanging out and talking shop was the getting-to-know-one-another phase of their relationship, the consummation, as it were, came out of shared vulnerability. The two ended up in a writing group, sharing material back and forth. That’s when they finally read one another’s work, and their perspective shifted. Kelly, who had come to USC from small town roots in both Hawaii and Washington state without much of a plan before the screenwriting program took him in, was similarly moved by Lanzing’s work. “I'm realizing the story he wrote was my experience, he says. “This is a brother, not a foe. Embrace him like a brother. From that moment on, that respect changed the dynamic of our relationship.”

The pair spent more and more time together, especially after they graduated and took a road trip to Lollapalooza to see Radiohead—which Kelly wryly describes as “pots and pans being tossed down the stairs music”—where their respect for one another grew. “I have so much respect for who he is as a creator, " Kelly says. “It's almost like they say, Don't meet your heroes, but it's like, I kind of just get to hang out with one of my heroes all the time.” Lanzing quickly chimes in, saying, “ I just had never met somebody who thought the way Collin did . . . I learned pretty early, not as a writing partner, but as a friend, he filled in a lot of my gaps.”

In those early days, the pair didn’t always have the language to express the depths of their friendship to one another, with both agreeing it was easier to pour that into the work and have it manifest there.

“We were able to have that rich emotional relationship without saying those words exactly because we were able to funnel it all through our characters,” Kelly adds. “We were almost forced by the fact that we had pledged our careers to each other. We were like, the only way we're going to get through this life is together, making beautiful stories together, making things that are tragic and real and sincere. That necessitates us opening up and being real and tragic and sincere with each other in a way that's like, whoa, whoa, whoa, whoa, this is just for the story, man.”

THE AFOREMENTIONED LOLLAPALOOZA trip turned out to be beneficial not only in furthering the pair’s friendship, but giving them a real foot in the door in Hollywood. As they drove from Los Angeles to Chicago, the boys started riffing on story ideas, eventually hitting on their shared love of Akira Kurosawa movies and Japanese culture. On the road, they put together an outline for a movie called Sundown about seven samurai defending a town from Gaki, aka Japanese vampires. “Ultimately, it's about this guy having to kill his best friend,” Lanzing says. “We didn’t really realize we were writing a lot of the early parts of our relationship into the script.”

By the time the trip back to LA concluded, the duo thought Sundown was in good-but-not-great shape. Kelly had a deal with William Morris, and brought the script to his agent there with the understanding that any sale involving the story was now a package deal. While the movie ultimately never got made, it made enough of an impression to get them recurring work, even as the industry shifted in real time, often being tapped to work on web series, which became all the rage after the 2008 financial crisis.

The two worked part-time on storytelling while holding full-time jobs. That schedule meant they’d get together for face-to-face working sessions on Thursday and all day Sunday. “Our wives, our friends, everybody had to accept the fact that, look, if you want Jack or Collin on [those days], you don't get them,” Kelly says.

With such a complicated working arrangement, the possibility of not working as a duo did come up. At one point, Kelly took a chance to co-write an animation with another writer friend, but the production ended up spanning six different time zones. Fundamentally, the project was messing and began taking a toll on Kelly, to the point where it finally came to a head.

“It genuinely is one of the only times I’ve gotten angry at Collin,” Lanzing adds. “That’s the one time I’ve ever had to deploy it in our relationship because it had literally gotten to the point where it was like, he’s disappearing. Not just into this project but into himself.”

Lanzing found it to be the “scariest” moment in their entire relationship. “I think that is legit one of the first times we were ever really emotionally vulnerable with another,” he says. “The crazy thing is, I think it actually brought us closer together.”

“Oh, yeah,” Kelly interjects. “I was scream crying.”

“We lost it at each other and were able to put it back together,” Lanzing says, picking the thread back up. “I think it taught us both, like, okay, we can do this.”

A FEW YEARS ago, DC Comics published a series of Batman-related one-shots. The series, titled “One Bad Day,” draws inspiration from Alan Moore, Brian Bolland, and John Higgins’ seminal The Killing Joke story. In the story, the Joker rolls out his MO, a fitting bit of text that’s become totemic within comic book lore: “All it takes is one bad day to reduce the sanest man alive to lunacy. That’s how far the world is from where I am. Just one bad day.”

The one-shots featured a handful of stories, all told from the point of view of a Batman villain. Kelly and Lanzing’s story, drawn by Xermanico, follows the Basil Karlo version of Clayface, who is on the verge of a breakout role and slowly descends into madness as he kills more and more people in the film industry to get what he wants. It’s also clearly one of the main sources of inspiration for the DCU’s new body horror film Clayface, which released earlier this month to the excitement of DC fans (and landing at the top of the box office). The film follows a version of Clayface named Matt Hagen (Tom Rhys Harries) and his quest for continued fame on the silver screen (even with plenty of body mutations and murders coming along the way).

Kelly says that DC reached out to them specifically because of their Hollywood background—and thought that would be perfect for Clayface. “Talk about taking our trauma and doing our therapy—that book is taking all the pain that we've had and expressing it through the character and taking him through something really genuinely emotional and horrifying,” Kelly says. “And it has nothing to do with being a Clayface.”

But for both men, the book is another kind of cautionary tale. It’s what could have happened to them if they hadn’t found one another. “[Basil] kills and impersonates his supposed best friend,” Lanzing says. “That's the pivot point. That's the thing that makes it not Collin and I's story. Rather than look at the other guy and be like, I want the jobs you have, I want the opportunities you have, I want the talent you have, I want the ideas you have, I want the life you have, I want the face you have. Rather than doing any of that, what we did was we said, What if we take the monetary sacrifice involved in splitting that fifty-fifty, but then do it together and make an alliance that doesn't break, that doesn't lead to one of us stuffing the other one in the back of a car? Does that lead to a better outcome? “At this point, we’re getting to the part of the summit that seems like it led to a better outcome.”

Based on the expansive depth and breadth of their work, the answer is a resounding yes. But in how the two men talk about one another, and their friendship, and what they’ve meant to one another, even without creating together, it’s the kind of relationship anyone would love to have. A perfect partnership where each brings out the best in the other, resulting in something that transcends and goes beyond—even if maintaining and cultivating that relationship can sometimes get tricky or rocky, it’s worth it. Being in a foxhole with someone else is always going to be better than doing it alone.

#4902. Where to Buy Vintage Lamps (2026): 1stDibs, Etsy, and More

Listen—we’ve all fallen prey to the sort of lighting lethargy that keeps our hastily-purchased mass market table lamps on our bedside. A fluorescent task lamp might still hover over the couch, a garish flush mount shedding its harsh light. So why a vintage lamp? Because it indicates you thought about your lighting instead of going the route of a last-minute, add-to-cart impulse buy. This was deliberately searched and purchased. This was a choice made with historical design context in mind, whether it’s midcentury modern, Art Deco, or Hollywood Regency. A carefully considered piece of lighting shows your home decor chops—you’ve gone above and beyond the look of the room to consider the vibe of the room. A magnificent vintage piece communicates all that. We rounded up a few retailer options to find just the thing you need for a living room revamp, a ceiling light overhaul, or a lampshade upgrade.

We pull from 1stDibs regularly when shopping out our various home tours, where we can find the iconic works sprinkled throughout the homes of celebrities, designers, and celebrity designers. A Charlotte Perriand sconce indicates at least one substantial coffee book on the oeuvre of Le Corbusier is nearby, and a reissue of the 1960’s Gelato lamp by Italian designer Carlo Nason’s are just the tip of the iceberg. In other words: Design dorks will delight in this catalog of artifacts and iconic pieces.

Etsy is a go-to when you’re searching for a vibe rather than a specific model or designer. Try keyword searching for terms like “Art Deco” or “ginger jar lamp” and a trove of collectibles, like thrift-store-friendly finds and veritable antique gems, will surface in search. The online marketplace’s vast web of sellers means a library of vintage lighting that spans centuries and styles, often times for less than what you’d find at places like 1st Dibs and Chairish since sellers choose what they price for shipping. Contributor Lori Keong has had good luck buying pieces there from across the pond in the UK and even further afield in Slovenia, with lots of vintage sellers from the states like Philly’s Betsu and Chicago’s Spears Furnishings selling their wares on Etsy as well.

eBay brought me such things as this vintage Tarogo frog made from Ichiban rice paper that I passed up in a thrift store in Maine and thought about for months before it surfaced on the secondhand retailer. Now he sits proudly on an armoire overlooking my dining room. Which is to say: Every vintage table lamp that’s ever existed is somewhere in their backlog. Chinoiserie, vintage Noguchi lamps, kitschy conch shell lamps, Tiffany glass shades, the OG Pixar lamp—it’s all there.

Design Within Reach regularly re-issues iconic models in collaboration with the original manufacturers, giving second life to pieces like Verner Panton’s Panthella (1971), Herman Miller’s 1952 Cigar Lotus floor lamp, Greta Grossman’s 1947 Gräshoppa, and the Space Age-y Nessino table lamp which debuted in 1967, to name a few.

Rejuvenation’s vintage collection is part of a larger initiative to prevent waste. The brand’s Portland-based restoration studio team finds and restores over 3,000 antiques around the world every year to help keep them out of landfills. While the vintage pieces are one-of-a-kind finds, a large selection mirrors Rejuvenation’s midcentury modern-inspired offerings, like 20th-century brass lamps and designs with floral and gilt detailing.

Abask has some of the most beautiful antique Murano glassware lamps we’ve seen to date. Case in point: Albano Poli’s stained glass creation pictured below. The artist got his claim to fame for collaging colored glass across geometric lighting fixtures. Modern glass lamps like this one are easy to come by across Abask’s collections, as well as minimalist metal lamps and hand-painted ceramic lamps, if those better suit your style.

#4896. 11 Best Love Seats for Small Spaces That Are Stylish (2026)

The most inviting living spaces have comfortable, functional seating options—but you don’t need a room big enough for a sprawling sectional to establish that welcoming vibe. Instead, turn to a small-space–friendly love seat, a compact sofa designed for two people, hence the name.

Even if you don’t live in a tiny apartment, a small sofa can add a romantic je ne sais quoi to a room, making a space feel more intimate, especially if you select a style with a curvy backrest that envelops guests like a hug. If you do have the space, pairing your love seat with a chaise or accent chairs can extend the footprint and add even more seating options while providing more opportunity to elevate the room’s design. One look at the vintage love seat that lives in Usher’s Atlanta home will make you a believer.

Here, find 11 designs, all between 55 and 70 inches wide, to accommodate all tastes, including the iconic mod Knoll womb settee and the more traditional leather love seat from Crate & Barrel.

Why settle for a traditional silhouette when you can have a bit of fun with your love seat? The West Elm Bogart style caught contributor Lori Keong’s eye because of its elegant bolstered back with rounded arms and a sculptural cutout base that conceals the legs. “No blocky, chubby legs or ugly hairpin appendages to stare at while I vacuum….what a concept!” she says in her review. Initially, she had some reservations about it being too stiff, classifying it as feeling similar to a medium-firm mattress. “It’s definitely firm and supportive but also not rock-hard, with just enough give—and no sag—to sleep or sit on comfortably.” Keong chose the alabaster basketweave, but if that color-upholstery combination doesn’t fit your space or style, now you can choose from 79 other options.

Serena & Lily wears the crown for coastal decor, but its offerings extend far beyond predictable patterns of sky pinstripes and gingham. With more than 125 upholstery options available—including linen, basketweave, cotton, denim, and more—in solid colors and patterns, the Spruce allows you to dress up the classic silhouette to fit a home of any style. If the slipcover is not your thing, the alternative is introducing exposed wood legs in four colorways. Regardless of your decor, the solid wood frame of this compact love seat is built to last.

For its boucle love seat, Quince uses performance fabric that is Oeko-Tex–certified, a designation that the textile in question is free of dangerous levels of harmful substances. Beneath the cozy, nubby fabric, foam cushions are supported by a hardwood frame, which is FSC-certified—a credential from the Forest Stewardship Council that specifies responsible wood sourcing. The combination of curvy, clean lines, and ivory color gives this two-seater an elevated elegance without a hefty price tag.

The Crate & Barrel Axis love seat comes in an astounding 45 leather options, including more traditional hues like caramel and bold burgundy and statement-making onyx. Silicone-based vegan materials are also available. The look, anchored by track arms and an angled back, is classic and can work just as well in an entryway as it would in a den.

The Pottery Barn Cameron power-reclining sofa may make you forget that you opted for a love seat at all. Once you hit the power button to recline and stretch out, you’ll forget all about this modular sofa’s compact stature. If your space can accommodate a few extra inches, consider adding a storage compartment between the seats for a spot to stash a mid-movie snack. Plus, the modular nature of this couch’s design means it can shift and grow with you, should you decide to place it elsewhere in your home or move to a new space altogether.

The Sabai Essential love seat looks like a hug. The low-profile seat with bench seating and plush back cushions practically begs for late-night reading sessions. Lounging is also sweeter when you know that the recycled polyester velvet is Oeko-Tex–certified as well as Global Recycled Standard 4.0-verified, a certification that affirms the content of recycled materials. And if you end up loving the seat too much, you can make use of the brand’s Repair Don’t Replace program that allows you to revive various components, like a single cushion or a scuffed sofa leg. Alternatively, if you prefer to refresh the upholstery du jour, simply swap it for a new slipcover at a fraction of the cost of a new love seat.

Joybird is the place to find a modern love seat, especially one that will go with your colorful wallpaper. The Amelia style is curvy with a twist: Its 1970s-inspired frame has a suave shape, but the cushions retain the expected rectangular configuration. Available in an eye-popping 91 colors, you should do yourself a favor and order the swatch sample box to help you choose. At 56.5 inches wide, it’s one of the smaller love seats on this list, making it ideal for teeny-tiny alcoves and nooks.

The AllModern Rae love seat comes in velvet and linen textures, giving you flexibility on whether you prefer a dressier or more casual style. Regardless of the upholstery choice, the channel stitching detail on the arms gives this compact love seat a contemporary look. The fabric is stain- and spill-resistant, but it is spot-clean–only. However, its reversible cushions let you hide a one-off oops. Also nice is that at just under 100 pounds, it’s a relatively easy sofa to move around by yourself. Toss in a few throw pillows, and you’ve got a whole new look.

Just looking at the CB2 Deseo makes us want to flop down and relax atop the two giant, pillow-like cushions filled with feathers and down. With a whopping 82 options for upholstery type and color, you can customize the love seat to fit into any room: Go for an opulent look with a rich merlot color, or tropical with the lsla Royal banana leaf linen-cotton for your sunroom.

Knoll earmarked a spot in the history of design with the Womb settee by Finnish architect Eero Saarinen, who said in 1948, “Today, more than ever, we need to relax.” Nearly 80 years later, the statement still rings true. It took Saarinen two years to make this chair, but from there, production didn't stop. Adding this piece to your furniture collection basically makes it an heirloom.

Take a page out of Brooklyn Decker and Andy Roddick’s Charlotte, North Carolina, entryway that features a small sofa with a back made of unexpected arches. The Lulu and Georgia Aimee Song Neve sofa surprises with its artfully sculpted seats that—at a medium on the firmness scale—are all about comfort. Among many upholstery and color options, it’s available in chunky boucle and garnet-and-blue stripe—a color combo that’s designer-approved.

#4893. Ferrari Luce EV starts at $560,800 in the US, $80K less than expected

Ferrari’s first electric car, the Luce, will start at $560,800 in the US, with American deliveries planned for the second quarter of 2027. Car and Driver first reported the figure, and Ferrari has confirmed it.

That’s roughly $80,000 under the ~$640,000 number that has followed the Luce since its unveiling in May, including in our own coverage. But Americans aren’t getting a deal.

Ferrari didn’t give a US price when it launched the Luce in Rome on May 25. It gave a European one: €550,000. Most outlets, us included, ran that through the exchange rate of the day, about $1.16 per euro, and got to roughly $640,000.

The problem is that European car prices are normally quoted with VAT included, and Italy’s rate is 22%.

Take the tax out and the Luce costs about €450,800 in Italy. At today’s rate of roughly $1.12 per euro, that’s around $506,000. So the US price is about $55,000 higher than what an Italian buyer pays before taxes, and American buyers still owe state sales tax on top of the $560,800.

Some of that gap is trade policy. European-built cars now face a 15% US tariff, and Ferrari already raised prices on part of its US lineup last year to cover it. The $560,800 also doesn’t include options, and Ferrari hasn’t published what those cost. Nobody buys a base Ferrari.

None of this will slow sales. Ferrari sold out its 2026 Luce allocation of just under 500 units in less than two months, with China doing a lot of the heavy lifting. In August, the first production chassis went for $40 million at a charity auction for the Ferrari Foundation.

That’s despite a rough reveal. The design, done with Jony Ive and Marc Newson’s LoveFrom, got hammered online, and Ferrari’s stock dropped sharply in the days after the launch before recovering.

The hardware is serious. The Luce uses four motors, one per wheel, for 1,035 hp combined. It’s built on an 800V architecture with a 122 kWh battery pack from SK On, charges at up to 350 kW, and Ferrari rates it at 529 km (329 miles) WLTP. It does 0-100 km/h in 2.5 seconds and weighs 2,260 kg (4,982 lb). It’s also Ferrari’s first five-seater.

For comparison, a Lucid Air Sapphire with over 1,200 hp costs around $250,000. You could buy two and still have change left over.

"None of this will slow sales. Ferrari sold out its 2026 Luce allocation of just under 500 units in less than two months, with China doing a lot of the heavy lifting."

This only proves that the world has too many mega wealthy people with more money than brains. All while the middle class continues to shrink and more people are headed towards homelessness.

The bigger story is that the price clearly doesn’t matter to Ferrari’s buyers. Plenty of luxury brands have convinced themselves that wealthy buyers won’t pay supercar money for an electric car. Maserati killed the electric MC20 Folgore over exactly that fear. Ferrari just put a $560,800 sticker on a four-door EV with a polarizing design and sold out a year of production anyway.

We’re not convinced every one of those orders comes from love of the car. Ferrari’s allocation game rewards loyal customers, and some buyers are surely taking a Luce to stay in line for the next limited-run V12. But a deposit is a deposit.

The real test comes in 2027 and 2028, once the first wave of collectors has its cars and the Luce has to sell on its own merits. Will it?Personally, I hope that the Luce leads to Ferrari trying to make some electric sports cars.

If you’re buying an EV, even one that costs a lot less than a Ferrari, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4890. Matt Damon and David Letterman Pop into The Gilded Age Just for Funzies

HBO’s period drama got a very modern surprise from two very recognizable faces.

IF YOU’RE NOT familiar with HBO’s The Gilded Age, it’s a pretty easy show to explain. Set in New York City during the titular era—the rapidly changing 1880s—the show is an exploration of the rich-people trials and tribulations of a new money family (led by Carrie Coon and Morgan “Train Daddy” Spector) and their neighbors, an old money family (led by Christine Baranski).

One of the show’s most important aspects is its production design, with intricately built sets and elaborately planned costumes helping to transport its viewers back nearly 150 years. Immersion is the name of the game.

With a setup like that, The Gilded Age is just about the last show where you’d expect to see a couple of our most recognizable modern stars walk directly across the screen—but that’s just what Matt Damon and David Letterman did, less than 10 minutes into the show’s season 4 premiere.

Damon, the Oscar-winning movie star who earlier this year led Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey and Netflix’s The Rip, and Letterman, the legendary talk show host, walked across the screen in full costume, saying nothing, in a blink-and-you’ll-miss-it moment.

As it turns out, according to a story published by Vulture, the whole thing was born from Letterman’s lifelong desire to make a surprise appearance in something as a background actor, something that began way back in 1977 when he saw a fellow comedian friend slyly appear in the background of a scene in Annie Hall.

In recent years, Letterman has apparently come close to shooting background scenes in Wes Anderson’s The Phoenician Scheme, the upcoming Michael B. Jordan-directed version of The Thomas Crown Affair, and Black Rabbit alongside Jason Bateman—but for one reason or another, none of them came to be.

Letterman filmed an upcoming episode of his current show, the Netflix interview series My Next Guest Needs No Introduction with David Letterman, with Damon, and discovered that Damon and Ben Affleck began their careers as background actors, and, for the sake of the bit, figured this would be the perfect opportunity to see his dream come to fruition. “We thought, Okay, this could be the perfect opportunity,” Next Guest showrunner Isabel Richardson explained to Vulture. “It’d be really fun for Dave to do this with Academy Award–winner Matt Damon, and it tied into his origin story really well.”

Damon, luckily, was into the idea too—and they made it happen. “It was a kind of hysterical moment when our production supervisor Leah Baker came up to me on set and was like, ‘Well, I have a kind of funny, interesting question: Would you like Dave Letterman and Matt Damon to be background on our set one day?’” Gilded Age showrunner Churchill said in the same Vulture story. “I just started laughing.”

Surprise appearances and cameos have long been a part of Damon’s playbook. Marvel fans certainly remember his brief appearances in movies like Deadpool 2 and Thor: Ragnarok, while fans of Damon’s frequent collaborator Steven Soderbergh’s movies won’t forget his surprise turns in movies like No Sudden Move and Che: Part Two. He even appeared in a very brief role in Ethan Coen’s Drive Away Dolls after working with Ethan and his brother Joel on the 2010 hit True Grit.

Perhaps Damon’s most memorable (and truly most random) cameo comes in the 2004 comedy Eurotrip, though. He appears early in the film as Donnie, the lead singer of an alt-rock band who sings a song about the affair he was apparently having with the main character’s girlfriend. “Scotty Doesn’t Know” is a pillar of both 2000s pop-punk and that era’s teen sex comedy movement, and Damon’s completely out-of-nowhere cameo is a huge reason why.

And, look, HBO seems to enjoy this kind of thing anyway. Just last month, we spotted the University of Nebraska’s head football coach Matt Rhule in the final episode of Lanterns. Does his appearance make sense in a show set in Nebraska? Sure. Did we ever expect to see Matt Rhule in an acclaimed, highly watched HBO drama set in a superhero world? No, we certainly did not.

It’s fun (and probably a bit trippy at first) to come to grips with seeing who you think you’re seeing. And if, somehow, you don’t know who either of those two are, you probably won’t notice at all and won’t think a thing. And isn’t that how all great cameos should go, really?

#4889. Tesla now says it needs its own maps for FSD after years of dismissing maps

Tesla says it’s building its own maps to fix some of the most common problems with its “Full Self-Driving” system, including reading speed limits, parking, and choosing routes. The company’s head of AI described the effort on Tesla’s Q3 2026 earnings call.

It’s a notable shift for a company whose CEO spent years arguing that self-driving systems relying on maps are brittle. And it shows Tesla’s fleet is now doing the same job Waymo’s cars do: mapping the roads so the self-driving system knows what it can’t reliably figure out on its own.

Tesla fans argue that Waymo’s approach differs because it creates HD maps with lidar, but ultimately both are creating their own maps; if your argument is that Tesla’s maps are less well-defined and needed for driving for different reasons than Waymo’s, you don’t really have an argument.

The question came from Tesla’s shareholder Q&A. A retail investor wrote that mapping “continues to be a problem” for FSD, listing speed limits, parking and typical routes as the three biggest issues, and asked why Tesla relies on third-party maps instead of its own.

Tesla’s VP of AI, Ashok Elluswamy, agreed that “we shouldn’t be depending on external people’s maps.” He said Tesla is building its own maps using a new AI-based mapping and navigation architecture, fed by what the fleet collectively learns, that will also learn individual drivers’ preferences and respond to voice commands.

He said the technology will start handling the beginning and end of trips with FSD v15, and over the following months take over navigation, parking and destination handling.

Tesla has gone back and forth on this before.

In 2015, when it launched Autopilot, Tesla told Fortune it had started building high-precision maps with “100 times the level of granularity” of standard navigation, using data from its customer cars.

At its Autonomy Day in 2019, Elon Musk said Tesla had tried high-precision lane maps and realized it was “a huge mistake.” He called relying on them a “really bad idea” that makes a self-driving system “extremely brittle,” in the same presentation where he called lidar a “fool’s errand.” The message was clear: Waymo needed lidar and detailed maps, and Tesla would solve driving with cameras and AI alone, anywhere.

Seven years later, Tesla is building a map layer from its own fleet to fix the things its vision system gets wrong.

To be fair to Tesla, this isn’t an HD map in the sense Musk criticized. Tesla has always used navigation maps, and building its own instead of licensing them isn’t a contradiction on its own.

But look at the problems Tesla is trying to fix. Speed limits, parking and routes are all about understanding the environment, and FSD still often fails at it, especially with speed limit signs.

Last month, a Belgian road safety group found that FSD sped in 55% of the 30 km/h zones it tested in Brussels, averaging 44 km/h, partly because the car’s screen showed a 50 km/h limit in most of those zones. In the US, NHTSA is weighing an investigation into claims that Tesla Robotaxis speed through active school zones, covering about 1,000 Model Y and Cybercab vehicles.

A speed limit sign is about as simple as road information gets. If an end-to-end vision system can’t reliably read and apply it, and the fix is a better map, then vision alone isn’t doing the job Tesla said it would.

That brings Tesla closer to Waymo than it would like to admit.

Waymo maps a city by driving its sensor-equipped vehicles through it before launching service, and keeps those maps updated as its fleet drives. Tesla is now describing the same idea: its cars collect what they see, and that data feeds a Tesla-built map that helps every car drive better. The main difference is that Waymo’s cars have lidar on top.

It also fits how Tesla’s Robotaxi service actually operates: city by city, inside small geofenced service areas, with Tesla handling the fleet operations. Tesla isn’t talking about centimeter-level HD maps today, but a fleet-built map that keeps getting more detailed could easily head that way.

There’s nothing wrong with using maps. Every serious self-driving program does, and a map built from millions of Tesla cars could be a real advantage. If it fixes FSD’s speed limit problems, that’s good news for everyone sharing the road with it.

Add this as another reason why unsupervised FSD will not be going out to consumer cars in Q2 of 2027. There will be more reasons forthcoming, but not for some time as the main focus will be raking in more cash for those payday goals.

What’s wrong is the years of messaging that Tesla’s approach didn’t need any of this. Musk sold FSD as a general solution that would work anywhere through vision alone, while dismissing Waymo’s maps and lidar as crutches. Now Tesla is adding the map, it already operates in geofences, and the AI still can’t reliably read a 30 km/h sign in Brussels.

Waymo built its maps and has been running a driverless service for years. Tesla is building its maps now and has pushed unsupervised FSD in customer cars to Q2 2027, and only inside its Robotaxi fleet. At what point do we admit that both companies are solving the same problem in very similar ways, and only one of them has lidar?

We are a decade into Tesla’s self-driving effort and it is doing exactly what Musk used to say doesn’t count as a “true self-driving system”: geo-fenced areas using maps.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. #4888. Jaylen Brown Is Keeping His Boston Skyrise Apartment—and Its 18th-Century Harp Too

Jaylen Brown has an 18th-century harp sitting in his living room, and that harp rules. Having a harp in your home as a newly 30-year-old man, with a professional harpist who appears when the mood strikes said 30-year-old man, is an outstanding use of free will.

“Most of the time I use my harp, it’s just me and my chef,” Brown says. A memory raced through my head: me at the end of my twenties, with a harp (broken) in my unit. And how central it would be to the social hours of 10 p.m. to 4 a.m.

Brown’s harp is not that. “It’s almost like therapy sessions,” he says.

Jaylen Brown with his cat Forky in the speakeasy of his Boston apartment design by Linda Zarifi of Zarifi Design. The project construction is by Core Group Boston, and the art advisory is by Miranda Girard. The sofa is by Charles Zana from The Invisible Collection and the ottoman is also from The Invisible Collection.

The artwork in the speakeasy is by Francisco Valverde and flanked with sconces by Apparatus. The curtain fabric is by Dedar, and the star ceiling lights are by UFO Lighting.

The wallcovering in the speakeasy is by Gregorius Pineo.

He’s a different dude in a way that makes him singular, both as a member of the National Basketball Association and as a young and famous Black man in the United States of America.

On July 1, 2026, hundreds of television talkers said “the basketball world has been turned upside down,” because Brown’s years as a Celtic were over. After nine years as half of Boston’s dynamic duo with Jayson Tatum, he was traded to Philadelphia. “It’s no beef,” Jaylen politely told reporters after a victory, beard absolutely glistening. “Might be a little lamb chops and gravy. He should stay ready at all times.” 23 days later, Brown’s new teammate became LeBron James, creating one of the strongest starting fives in NBA history. You love to see someone find a nice new gig. But as for where he rests his head, Brown held on to his place in Boston.

In the foyer, the walls are painted in Ashland Slate by Benjamin Moore. The custom table holds Brown’s championship trophy.

In the living area, the Hamilton sofa by Minotti surrounds coffee tables by Carlyle Collective. The floor lamp is by Tom Dixon and the harp is antique.

“I grew up here, age 19 to 29,” he says. “I developed here in Boston as a young man.” The feeling is mutual; October 24, his birthday, was named Jaylen Brown Day by the city. But the crib itself is doing a lot of the pulling. “There’s been other places, but this one–I created it, it was crafted by my vision,” he says. “This one felt the most like home.”

He hired a team to bring his dreams to life. Linda Zarifi, of Zarifi Design, was his interior designer and collaborator. “The first thing he said was ‘I don’t want a dining table,’” Zarifi says. “And I’m like, ‘Where are you going to…eat?’”

The answer was a 22-foot banquette, built for most home activities. “I wanted to open the space up. I wanted gold. I wanted a see-through wine cellar. She went and found it all,” Brown says.

The custom dining banquette is upholstered in Crescendo Suede by Kravet and the glass and metal tables are also custom by Jamie Cumming. The TG-10 Sling dining chairs are by Gratz Industries and the wall sconces are by Gubi. Inside the frame on the wall is a proclamation from the city of Boston for Jaylen Brown Day.

Brown in the dining 22-foot banquette playing chess.

The result is a 4,000 square-foot apartment overlooking Boston’s Financial District. Brown purchased the downtown space at the end of 2023 and reworked it over the last two years. Outside, the apartment is a brief 15 minute walk from the TD Garden, home to Brown’s former team, the Boston Celtics. Inside, Italian marble bathroom floors, 24-karat fixtures that Brown calls “jewelry for the bathroom,” a custom suede pool table, a party-ready speakeasy, and that see-through wine cellar.

There’s also the medical hyperbaric chamber one step from his bed, one of the truest signs that he is a single man.

Jaylen is figuring it out in real time, and he’s letting us watch and listen.

The trophy case in the speakeasy is a custom design by Zarifi and fabricated by Jamie Cumming of Loki Custom Furniture.

A shelf in the speakeasy holds a collection of Brown’s memorabilia.

A chandelier by John Pomp hangs over the bar area with stools by Blackman Cruz upholstered in a leather by Holly Hunt.

Kashmir barstools from Resident tuck under the kitchen counter. The flushmounts are from RH and the kitchen cabinets are painted in Cheating Heart by Benjamin Moore.

They say he’s not new to this. In 2020, five days after George Floyd was murdered, he drove 15 hours from Boston to our hometown of Atlanta to lead a march. The same guy who took the swooshes off his Nikes and now has his own shoe company (with custom closets by Molteni&C to house his own collection). And the same guy who uses this home to stream with Lil Baby and to display art and artifacts dedicated to his family.

“The things that he would splurge on are the things that mattered most to him,” Zarifi says. There’s the commissioned portrait in cracked glass by Simon Berger of his grandmother in the foyer. “For him, his flex was his harp.”

Brown seated on a bench in the primary bedroom. The custom bed is upholstered in a fabric by Mokum Textiles. The pendants by Apparatus hang over custom nightstands flanking the bed.

“I had them recreate the bedding and the comforter of the Four Seasons,” Brown says. The bedding is custom from Crem & Co. Brown’s hyperbaric chamber is beside the bed.

“I love the tempered glass,” Brown says. The closet design is the Glis Closet System by Molteni&C. The pendant light is by Henge.

The custom vanity in the primary bathroom is flanked with sconces by Articolo. The fittings are by Dornbracht.

The tub and counters are topped in a Pietra Grey stone and the ceiling is painted Ashland Slate by Benjamin Moore.

The Sally armchair in the primary bedroom is by Minotti and covered in Holly Hunt fabric.

He hasn’t taken lessons yet, good for no more than an impassioned glissando. What he does do, however, is soak in the sounds from Li Shan Tan, the principal harp for the New Haven Symphony Orchestra, who plucks from his palace. “From time to time, when she is not around, I go and I try to recreate some sounds.”

#4885. BMW is already discounting the new iX5 with a $1,000 loyalty bonus

The iX5 is set to go on sale in early 2027, but BMW is already offering a loyalty discount to sweeten the deal for those looking to stay with the brand.

Following the new iX3 and i3, the iX5 will go on sale in early 2027 with BMW’s Neue Klasse-inspired design, technology, and sixth-generation (Gen6) eDrive system.

The iX5 is the first fully electric BMW X5. For its fifth generation, the BMW X5 will be available in gas, plug-in hybrid (PHEV), and battery electric (EV) powertrains in the US. Overseas markets will also see diesel and hydrogen fuel-cell versions.

While the gas-powered 2027 X5 40 xDrive is now available, the electric iX5 still says it’s “coming in 2027” on BMW’s website.

BMW is already sweetening the deal with a $1,000 loyalty discount for returning buyers or lessees, according to a memo CarsDirect reviewed that was sent to dealers.

Although a $1,000 credit may not seem like much, it’s twice what BMW is offering for the 2027 iX3. The discount doesn’t require a trade-in and can be stacked with other offers.

BMW’s loyalty discount provides a cash rebate off the MSRP when you buy or lease a new BMW through BMW Financial Services and have owned or leased another BMW within the last 12 months.

The iX5 doesn’t have any other current discounts or lease offers, but keep an eye out closer to launch.

Earlier this week, BMW showcased the i5’s fast-charging capabilities for the first time during a media event in Southern California with IONNA and Alpitronic. During the event, the iX5 60 xDrive hit a peak of 464 kW, which BMW said enables it to add up to 170 miles of range in 10 minutes.

The iX5 60 xDrive uses a massive 144 kWh battery, which BMW estimates will offer up to 435 miles of range.

Powered by a dual-motor all-wheel-drive powertrain, the electric SUV delivers a combined 578 hp (425 kW) and 593 lb-ft of torque, and can go from 0 to 60 mph in 4.4 seconds. It can also tow up to 6,000 lbs.

Inside, the iX5 adopts BMW’s Neue Klasse setup, with a wide 17.9″ central touchscreen and BMW Panoramic Vision that spans the base of the windshield. Buyers can also add the optional 14.6″ passenger screen.

Measuring 197.2″ in length, 78.7″ in width, and 68.9″ in height, the iX5 is about the size of the Porsche Cayenne Electric.

You have to wonder why Tesla’s name keeps coming up in this discussion, too. This BMW has a net battery capacity of 144 kWh and achieves an energy density Tesla hasn’t even come close to matching. Tesla wanted to get there, but the 4680 battery has been a technical disaster. Even the Cybertruck has a significantly smaller battery. On top of that, the BMW is highly efficient, thanks to its electrically excited synchronous motor (EESM), which doesn’t use rare earths. We’re talking about cutting-edge technology here, in a completely different league from Tesla.

BMW will officially launch the 2027 iX5 50 xDrive in the US in the first quarter of 2027, starting at $79,800.

The iX5 is the first all-electric BMW model produced in the US. Production is set to begin at its Plant Spartanburg in South Carolina by the end of the year.

Don’t want to wait for the bigger iX5? You can check out BMW’s smaller iX3 and see what’s available near you below.

#4881. This Picture-Perfect Suburban Kitchen Is an Argument for Maxed-Out Storage, Smart Appliances, and Custom Furniture

The house had everything a young family could want: classic Georgian architecture, a picturesque setting in East Glenview, Illinois, and a neighborhood where kids ride bikes and golden retrievers lounge in the sunshine. For a Chicago couple with three young sons, it checked nearly every box. The kitchen, however, was another story. Its awkward layout, dated appliances, and surprisingly scant storage made it feel ill-equipped for the demands of a family of five.

Fortunately, the problem was one that could be solved. Elizabeth Smolcich, principal interior designer of Glenview-based firm Mason & Brass, saw its potential. “We knew we’d have to do a heavy lift to make the kitchen feel like ours,” says homeowner Marie Gould. “Elizabeth had a vision for it that made it much more functional and kid-friendly for our family.”

Kitchen layout, before and after. A wider doorway improves the flow between kitchen and living room, while removal of a corner booth created space for a larger island and the potential for more seating.

Homeowner Marie Gould with her sons in the renovated kitchen

Smolcich started by widening the opening between the kitchen and living room just enough to let Gould easily eyeball what her boys, ages seven, six, and three, are up to while she’s cooking. And removing an awkwardly positioned corner booth, one that was too small to seat four comfortably but obtrusive enough to impede the 450-square-foot room’s flow, gave the designer enough space to expand and center a new kitchen island.

This island, topped with smoky quartzite, provides plenty of counter space for prepping meals or rolling out dough, and even has enough room for the boys to perch on when they want a close-up view of the action. Above the island, Smolcich installed schoolhouse-style fluted glass pendants, which the couple loved for their timeless appeal. The most significant addition came in the form of a custom banquette that looks built into the island but is actually a movable bench. The family slides this upholstered piece to one side of their dining table, which can expand from 66 inches to 96 inches, when they’re hosting a crowd. (They’re expecting more than 40 friends and family for Thanksgiving this year.)

This is a family that loves to entertain, says the designer. A double oven, then, was a necessity, as was a serious range that allows Gould to sear a steak with restaurant-quality char. Gould discovered the ultimate time-saving mom hack with her smart SKS range, which features a rapid preheat function and can boil a pot of water for pasta in just 60 seconds. It has both an induction cooktop to prevent tiny hands from burns as well as four gas burners that satisfy even the pickiest of eaters.

Two ovens were a necessity for the homeowners’ busy household. Brizo Rook faucets and Visual Comfort Parkington sconces, both in brass, warm up the space.

The designer flanked the stove with open shelving to keep oils and seasonings within easy reach. The dual gas and induction SKS range features Smart Knob technology.

“They’re big on texture,” says Gould of her family. “They love a sear and a proper crust, which is no problem with this.” (That said, she says she personally appreciates the range’s sous vide capability best, although the SKS Smart Knobs, with LED screens for each knob to monitor heat levels and time remaining, are a close second.)

Over the range Smolcich installed hidden, integrated built-in storage to hold Gould’s go-to oils and seasonings. “We took advantage of any and all real estate,” explains the designer. “We can make anything beautiful. That’s not the hard part of what we do. The hard part is making it wildly functional. If you feel like you have to go out of your way to retrieve something, you’re never going to love it.”

Designer Elizabeth Smolcich’s mood board of furniture, lighting, and surfaces

Hence the dual SKS dishwashers staggered from the Kohler prep sink in the island to the main Kohler sink (with a custom cutting board) on the perimeter; the pantry wall (painted Benjamin Moore Black Iron); an appliance garage (housing Gould’s toaster, blenders, mixer, and coffee maker); and a beverage bar with a 24-inch second fridge stocked with her kids’ juice boxes.

Prominently displayed is a treasured side-by-side picture frame with a photo of Gould’s parents baking with her oldest son next to a handwritten copy of their family’s banana bread recipe.

“That’s what this kitchen is to them,” says Smolcich. “In a few years, those boys will be sitting at that banquette doing their homework. We took this big house with a kitchen built for just a few people and made it one for a family.”

The beverage bar includes an under-the-counter refrigerator where the kids can grab their juice boxes.
